El Shaarawy: “Someone didn’t want Roma to win in Budapest.”

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Stephan El Shaarawy, currently at Genoa, gave an interview to La Gazzetta dello Sport, in which he discussed various topics, including the 2023 Europa League final he lost in Budapest while playing for Roma.

El Shaarawy on his decision to join Genoa and the impact De Rossi’s presence had on his former teammate in the capital: “We spoke a lot this summer. Things dragged on a bit due to some transfer market dynamics, but I continued training and we discussed our plans. I kept him updated on everything. Daniele’s presence had a significant impact. I had a substantial offer from Saudi Arabia, but I needed to feel alive again.”

The former Roma winger on DDR’s changes, from teammate to coach. “I found him just as I left him in Rome: a man of great character, a leader, that’s how I always recognized him in the locker room. He knows how to motivate, motivate, and tell the team what to say, always in the right way and at the right time. A great person and a well-prepared coach.”

Il Faraone and his rebirth in Rome after a difficult period in Monaco: “I discussed with my family whether it was the right choice, but for the last month and a half I trained alone, frustrated and disappointed again. Luckily, Roma arrived: a clear change, a rebirth with a back-heeled goal under the Sud on his debut.”

El Shaarawy spoke about the move to China and his new experience with the Giallorossi, which was marred by the defeat in the Europa League final: “I can’t deny that the financial aspect played a significant role in my decision. The experience wasn’t bad, but it was very difficult: Covid broke out, the league was suspended for seven months, and I couldn’t return to Shanghai due to quarantine. So I decided to end my relationship and return to Roma. Everything was perfect, except for that Europa League final that would have changed the club’s history… Someone didn’t let us.”

Bitcoin’s Current Drawdown Ahead of Anthropic IPO Mirrors SpaceX Listing — Why a $90K Rebound Could Be Coming by February

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin’s latest price decline may be setting the stage for a familiar market pattern, and history could give bulls reason to be optimistic. The benchmark cryptocurrency has suffered a sharp drop ahead of Anthropic’s upcoming red-hot stock market debut, echoing its 19% plunge in the 50 days leading up to SpaceX’s IPO earlier this year. However, if the historical pattern repeats, Bitcoin could be gearing up for a significant recovery, with a potential rebound toward $90,000 by February now drawing attention.

Strive CEO Matt Cole has observed an interesting historical parallel between Bitcoin’s current price action and its performance ahead of SpaceX’s record-breaking IPO. In the 50 days leading up to SpaceX’s $75 billion public debut on June 12, Bitcoin plunged 19%.

Now, with Anthropic preparing for a potential $64.4 billion IPO next week, BTC has posted a remarkably similar 18% decline over the same period, Cole noted in a post on X.

At press time, Bitcoin was trading at $64,665, according to CoinGecko, extending its recent losses after breaking below the $70,000 mark on November 13. The cryptocurrency continued its downward slide over the weekend, falling beneath $67,000 as bearish pressure intensified.

The Strive chief noted that overlaying the two price movements reveals an almost identical pattern, raising the possibility that Bitcoin could be repeating its pre-SpaceX sell-off ahead of another historic market debut.

Bitcoin extended its decline after SpaceX’s market debut, reaching its lowest point 18 days later before reversing course. The subsequent rally lifted BTC 28% above its IPO-day closing price within 100 days.

“If Anthropic follows the same pattern, most of the drawdown is behind us, and it could reverse quickly over the next couple of months,” Cole opined.

If history repeats itself, Bitcoin could climb toward $90,000 by late February, Cole suggested.

Binance co-founder Changpeng “CZ” Zhao also weighed in on the chart comparison, sharing the analysis on X and describing Cole’s findings as “interesting.”

However, the historical comparison comes with important caveats. Bitcoin’s sell-off ahead of the SpaceX IPO unfolded alongside market concerns over potential BTC sales by Michael Saylor’s Strategy, suggesting that factors beyond the anticipated listing may have contributed to the decline. As a result, attributing the entire downturn to IPO-related sentiment would overlook other pressures weighing on the market at the time.

Pfizer Will Pay Its 352nd Straight Quarterly Dividend on Dec. 7. Here's What It Would Take to Break the Streak.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pfizer's dividend streak spans 88 years, and it would take a perfect storm to end it.

By Keith Speights – Nov 19, 2026 at 4:44AM EST

Pfizer (PFE -1.44%) shareholders should mark Dec. 7, 2026, on their calendars. The giant drugmaker is scheduled to pay its 352nd consecutive quarterly dividend on that date.

That payout is a sure thing for investors who owned shares as of the close of business on Nov. 6, 2026. But what would it take to end Pfizer's impressive dividend streak?

No company can continue paying dividends indefinitely without generating enough cash to fund operations while funding the dividend program. Pfizer's free cash flow currently covers its dividend payouts. However, the big pharma company faces a patent cliff that could upset the status quo.

But Pfizer has a cushion that could allow it to pay quarterly dividends even if its free cash flow declines sharply. The drugmaker sits atop a cash stockpile of $14.3 billion (including cash, cash equivalents, and short-term investments). That's enough to fund dividends for well over a year. If Pfizer uses a big chunk of its cash to finance an acquisition, though, this cushion could disappear.

Importantly, Pfizer is counting on its newer drugs and promising late-stage pipeline candidates to help it successfully navigate the patent cliff. Multiple major clinical setbacks could cause management to reevaluate the company's growth prospects and potentially realize that continuing to pay dividends isn't viable.

It isn't too far-fetched to envision one of these scenarios causing Pfizer to cut its dividend. However, A reduced dividend would still keep the streak alive. I think that all three scenarios would have to unfold for Pfizer to eliminate its dividend.

Could this triple-whammy for Pfizer happen? It's possible, but not probable. In my view, Pfizer is much more likely to navigate the looming patent cliff relatively well, while continuing to generate sufficient free cash flow to fund the dividend at current levels. This big pharma stock should remain an attractive alternative for investors seeking higher income.

Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited Card Review: Is The $295 Fee Worth It?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Graphite™ Business Cash Unlimited Card launched in March 2026. On the surface, the card sounds rather uncompetitive — it has a $295 annual fee (Rates & Fees), and offers “just” 2% cash back on everyday spending, with limited bonus categories.

However, there are some things that could make this card worthwhile, including a potentially lucrative welcome offer, a solid return on spending, and some credits that can help offset the annual fee. If your business spends a lot, you value having an Amex, and you just want to earn simple cash back without a complex rewards structure, this could be a good option.

This post is my in-depth Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited review. Is the card worth it, and if so, under what circumstances? And what are the best alternatives?

Link: Learn more about the Graphite™ Business Cash Unlimited Card

Before I get into my Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited review, let me briefly summarize the key details of the card:

Beyond that, here are what I consider to be some of the major pros and cons of the Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited:

With the above out of the way, let’s look at the various aspects of the Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited, from the welcome offer, to the fees, to the rewards structure, to the other perks. This card is fairly straightforward, so there’s not all that much to understand, for better or worse.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited currently has a welcome offer where you can earn as high as $2,000 cash back after spending $50,000 within the first six months on eligible purchases. Reward Dollars can be redeemed as statement credits to offset transactions, or as a payment method for Amazon purchases.

Given that the card ordinarily offers at least 2% cash back, that means spending $50,000 could earn you a minimum of $3,000 (if offered the full bonus amount), equivalent to 6% cash back on that amount of spending, which is solid.

Admittedly the spending requirement is big (it averages out to $8,333 per month over the course of six months), but I do think businesses that spend a lot might find this to be achievable. Furthermore, it’s nice that you have six months to complete the spending requirement, while many other cards give you only three months to reach the spending requirement.

If you’re applying for the Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited, be aware of Amex’s general restrictions on applying for cards, including the “once in a lifetime rule.” That means you’re not eligible for the welcome offer on the card if you’ve already had this card, though you are potentially eligible for the bonus if you’ve had other Amex cards.

Anecdotally, I find Amex business cards to be quite easy to get approved for. I also love how with Amex, you can know if you’ll be approved with no credit score impact — the hard pull only happens after you’re told if you’re approved, and you accept the offer.

Also keep in mind that if you’re concerned about staying under Chase’s 5/24 limit, applying for an Amex business card won’t count toward that, since it doesn’t show up on your personal credit report in that way.

When it comes to credit score, there’s not a consistent credit score you need to get approved for the card, as it’s one of only many factors that determines eligibility. Personally, I’d hope to have a score in the “good” to “excellent” range, ideally 700+ points. However, people with lower scores have been approved, and people with higher scores have been rejected.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited has an annual fee of $295 (Rates & Fees), which is definitely on the steep side, especially given the relatively narrow appeal of the credits offered on the card, which require a lot of spending.

With the card’s 2% cash back rewards structure, you’d essentially recoup the annual fee (in terms of rewards) after $14,750 in spending.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited has an authorized user fee of $95 for the first 5 cards, and a $95 annual fee for each thereafter (Rates & Fees), though you can also get a no annual fee Employee Business Expense Card with no additional fee. Still, that’s a rather steep authorized user fee for a card that doesn’t actually offer many incremental perks. You can add up to 99 employee cards to your account.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited has no preset spending limit. The idea is that this gives you flexibility in ways that traditional credit cards don’t. The amount you can spend adapts based on factors such as your purchase, payment, and credit history. Rather than having a set limit, you could grow your purchasing power the more you consistently use your card and regularly make payments.

In fairness, it is also worth mentioning that not having a preset spending limit can also lead to some declines if the amount you can charge decreases, so it’s a little less predictable than a regular credit card. However, I find that more often than not, it works in the favor of cardmembers.

No Preset Spending Limit means your spending limit is flexible. Unlike a traditional card with a set limit, the amount you can spend adapts based on factors such as your purchase, payment, and credit history

In terms of its rewards structure, the Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited offers a minimum of unlimited 2% cash back on eligible purchases. On top of that, the card offers 5% cash back on flights and prepaid hotels booked through American Express Travel® Online. Just to do some very basic math:

All cash back on the card is earned as Reward Dollars, which can be redeemed as statement credits to offset transactions, or as a payment method for Amazon purchases. Unfortunately there are no other redemption options, so you couldn’t directly deposit the cash back into a business checking account, or anything like tht.

2% cash back on credit card spending is the “gold standard,” as I see it, so that’s excellent, and what everyone should be aiming for, at a minimum.

Meanwhile the 5% cash back on Amex Travel might sound appealing, but there are some catches. If you can route travel spending that way, it’s a good option, but the catch is that you may sometimes find lower rates elsewhere, so there could be an opportunity cost to booking that way.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited offers up to $2,400 in American Express One AP® statement credits, after spending $250,000 on eligible purchases on the card in a calendar year. Statement credits will be applied to the monthly bill charged to the card for Amex’s accounts payable solution, One AP, for the following calendar year, and this includes an auto-renewing platform access fee.

American Express One AP® is an accounts payable solution that can help transform the way your business pays suppliers. With One AP, you can streamline your supplier invoice payments on a single platform and pay by virtual card, check, and ACH. Enrollment is required.

This program charges a $200 monthly fee to pay vendors with non-cards, plus has a fee for checks and ACHs over a certain limit. So the $2,400 annual credit can potentially fully cover your monthly fee. While this is potentially a nice feature, keep in mind that many platforms already offer this as a built in feature at no extra cost, so it’s not really that special.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Unlimited offers the Pay Over Time feature, whereby cardmembers can carry a balance with interest on eligible charges, up to the Pay Over Time limit. The way this works, eligible charges will automatically be added to a Pay Over Time balance, up to the Pay Over Time limit.

You can choose to pay for these charges over time with interest, or you can pay your full balance each month, whichever is best for your business. You must pay in full any charge or a portion of a charge that is not added to a Pay Over Time balance.

Personally, I recommend paying the balance in full each billing cycle if possible, to avoid interest charges, especially given how high they can be. However, I think it’s worth clarifying that the Pay Over Time option is possible, given that this isn’t a traditional credit card.

It’s also worth emphasizing the difference between the Pay Over Time and the Pay In Full Limit. The latter could be an amount that has to be paid in full, and there’s no option to pay that over time with interest charges.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Card earns 2% cash back, and I consider that to be the minimum “gold standard” of credit card rewards. So the card’s rewards structure is perfectly fine. The 5% cash back on travel is also pretty limiting, given that you have to book through Amex.

I think the issue is the steep annual fee, given that there are also much more affordable (and even no annual fee) cards with that kind of a rewards structure. In fairness, Amex doesn’t have another business card earning unlimited 2% cash back, so this is at least a new product for the card issuer. It’s just not a new concept more broadly, and there are lots of competing cards that I’d consider to be superior.

So who should consider the card? As I see it, the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card is targeted at a specific demographic. This card could make sense for the following types of customers:

I do truly think this is a niche product. But the idea is that if you’re a small business owner charging hundreds of thousands or millions of dollars on credit cards per year, that annual fee won’t make much of a difference.

Still, I’d like to compare this to some other popular products, as I think there are other cards that may be a better fit for some other people.

Perhaps the best direct comparison to the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card is The American Express Blue Business Cash® Card (learn more). The card has no annual fee (Rates & Fees) and offers 2% cash back on the first $50,000 spent every calendar year (and 1% cash back thereafter).

The upside here is obvious, as you’re earning 2% cash back with no fee. The downside is that the welcome offer is much smaller (though also with a smaller spending requirement), the card has foreign transaction fees, you’re capped at earning 2% cash back on the first $50,000 spent annually, and the card does have a preset spending limit.

For a small business mostly spending money domestically, the Amex Blue Business Cash is a great alternative to the Amex Graphite Business Cash.

Another potential alternative to the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card is The Blue Business® Plus Credit Card from American Express (learn more). The card has no annual fee (Rates & Fees) and offers 2x Membership Rewards points on the first $50,000 spent every calendar year (and 1x points thereafter).

If you’re good at redeeming points, that’s a pretty incredible return, since based on my latest miles & points valuations, Amex points are worth 1.7 cents each, for a return of up to 3.4%. The downsides to the Amex Blue Business Plus are otherwise identical to those on the Amex Blue Business Cash — the welcome offer is much smaller (though also with a smaller spending requirement), the card has foreign transaction fees, you’re capped at earning 2x points on the first $50,000 spent annually, and the card does have a preset spending limit.

So the card is either useful if $50,000 covers your annual business spending, or you’re willing to use another card after you hit that initial $50,000 spending cap.

Speaking of premium Amex business cards, it’s also worth comparing the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card to the American Express® Business Gold Card (learn more). The Amex Business Gold has a $375 annual fee (Rates & Fees), and offers an incredible rewards structure (with up to 4x Membership Rewards points in select categories), plus statement credits that can greatly help offset the annual fee.

As I see it, the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card is the better option if you value cash back and a straightforward rewards structure, while the Amex Business Gold is the better option if you can take advantage of the bonus categories, and are willing to put in the effort to maximize points.

While the above comparisons are all to Amex cards, I think it’s also worth comparing the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card to the Capital One Spark Cash Plus (learn more), as it’s perhaps the most direct competitor.

The card has a massive welcome offer of $2,000 cash back after spending $30,000 within the first three months (plus an additional $2,000 cash back bonus for every $500,000 spent during the first year), offers 5% cash back on select Capital One Business Travel purchases and 2% cash back with no caps and no foreign transaction fees on other purchases, and it has a $150 annual fee. Furthermore, the card offers an annual bonus of $150 if you spend at least $150,000 on the card in a calendar year, essentially rebating the annual fee.

I do think the Capital One Spark Cash Plus is more compelling than the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card on balance, unless you specifically have a reason you’d like an Amex, or value the statement credits tied to spending $250,000 per year.

I’d also like to compare the Amex Graphite Business Cash Card to the Ink Business Premier® Credit Card (learn more), as it’s another potentially lucrative cash back card.

In addition to a big welcome offer of $1,000 cash back after spending $10,000 within the first three months, the card offers 5% cash back on Chase Travel purchases, 2.5% cash back on purchases of $5,000 or more, and 2% cash back on all other purchases, with no foreign transaction fees, and it has a $195 annual fee. So if you have a business that frequently makes large purchases, the Chase Ink Business Premier could be a great alternative.

The Amex Graphite Business Cash Card has a $295 annual fee, and offers unlimited 2% cash back, with no preset spending limit, plus a large welcome offer. The card also offers up to $2,400 in American Express One AP® statement credits, after spending $250,000 on eligible purchases on the card in a calendar year.

Ultimately I’d consider this to be a niche card. The card could be worth getting for the excellent welcome offer, and it could be worth it if you have a business that spends a huge amount, and values the lack of a preset spending limit, the card’s Amex branding, and/or the statement credits.

Beyond that, though, I tend to think there are better options. If you’re not a huge spender, the Amex Blue Business Cash and Amex Blue Business Plus are excellent options. If you’re willing to maximize points and bonus categories, consider the Amex Business Gold. And if you’re looking for the best direct alternative that’s arguably more lucrative, consider the Capital One Spark Cash Plus.

2 High-Yield Dividend Stocks Near Their 52-Week Lows That Income Investors Are Sleeping On

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These two stocks offer dividend yields well above the S&P 500.

By Lawrence Rothman, CFA – Nov 19, 2026 at 4:35AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Realty Income ›

Investors have reasons to sell stocks and push prices lower. Sometimes, they've become too short-term focused. For dividend investors, provided there aren't long-term fundamental issues, this presents an opportunity.

These two stocks trade near their 52-week low, but have a long history of prioritizing dividends. With their share prices down, investors can lock in a higher dividend yield, particularly relative to the average large-cap stock, which yields 1.1% in the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%).

Here's why income investors should strongly consider these high-yielding stocks.

General Mills (GIS -0.43%) shares closed at $34.68 on Nov. 17, near the 52-week low price of $31.18. In fact, the stock has dropped more than 25% over the last year and 46% over three years.

Investors haven't been thrilled with General Mills' sales, as price-conscious consumers seek lower-priced items. Sales have suffered due to lower volume.

General Mills' fiscal first-quarter sales, adjusted to remove sold businesses and foreign-currency translations, were flat versus a year ago. Lower volume subtracted 1 percentage point. The period ended on Aug. 30.

However, management has cut prices, and product volume has improved. It has been cutting costs to maintain profitability.

Meanwhile, General Mills has paid dividends for 127 straight years. It currently pays a quarterly rate of $0.61 per share, up $0.01 from last year. That works out to a 7% dividend yield.

Despite lower profitability hurting cash flow, General Mills still had enough free cash flow (FCF) to cover dividends. During its latest fiscal year, the company had FCF, or operating cash flow minus capital expenditures, of $1.6 billion. That compares to the $1.3 billion in dividends paid out.

Realty Income (O +0.41%) trades at about $58 compared to its 52-week low of $53.15. With the shares' recent rally, the real estate investment trust's (REIT) stock has gained 3.6% this year. Still, investors have grown a bit nervous about higher interest rates, which naturally affect high-dividend payers.

Meanwhile, Realty Income continues to post solid results. Occupancy rates continue to hover around 99%, and there was a 3.5% increase from expiring leases.

The company's adjusted funds from operations (AFFO), an important metric for REITs since it measures the cash available for distribution, has been steadily increasing. Most recently, Realty Income's third-quarter AFFO grew 3.7% year over year to $1.12 per share. With dividends at about 73% of this figure, it easily covered payouts.

Realty Income also has an impressive dividend history. It has raised payments for 117 straight quarters, and 137 times since the company's 1994 initial public offering. This month, it boosted its monthly dividend from $0.2715 per share to $0.274 per share.

Annualizing the new monthly rate yields a $3.288 per share dividend. That works out to a 5.7% dividend yield.

The Outlook - Week 11 (IND-HOU, MIA-BUF, NO-CHI, TEN-DAL)

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I'm not going to use the term "bye-pocalypse," but there are six teams on bye this week, taking more than a dozen fantasy-relevant players out of the pool. It's the only six-team bye week this season. Next week, we'll have ALL 32 teams playing during Thanksgiving week. This season has flown by so fast, and it's wild to think about where we are. It feels like just yesterday that we were coming out of drafts and Week 1 was imminent.

Now is the most important time of the fantasy season, with the fantasy football trade deadline (in most leagues) looming. You're doing everything you can to get the players you want while trying to shore up any weak spots on your roster. Once the trade deadline passes in your league, the best avenue for making those changes is the waiver wire, where the pickings are much slimmer. Either way, now is probably the best time to conduct a sweeping assessment of your roster and do what you have to do. That's where we come in: to help you make heads or tails of some of the options here. We'll do that for all 26 teams playing this week.

Block 1: IND-HOU, MIA-BUF, NO-CHI, TEN-DAL

Block 2: TB-DET, ARI-KC, BAL-CAR, PIT-PHI

Block 3: NYJ-LAC, LV-DEN, JAC-NYG, MIN-SF, CIN-WAS

"The Outlook" will give you the details, stats, analysis, recommendations and everything else you need to make informed fantasy football decisions. Every fantasy-relevant player on each team will be profiled in this article. We want everyone reading this to bring home championships in their fantasy leagues!

I'm incredibly excited to bring this article to all of you this season, and I hope you enjoy the ride. If you have any questions, feel free to find me on Bluesky (@ktompkinsii.bsky.social) or tag me in a post with any lineup questions. I'll do my best to answer them for you.

All game and implied point totals listed were compiled via DraftKings Sportsbook on Tuesday for IND-HOU, MIA-BUF, NO-CHI and TEN-DAL.

Teams on bye in Week 11: Atlanta Falcons, Cleveland Browns, Green Bay Packers, Los Angeles Rams, New England Patriots and Seattle Seahawks.

Implied Point Total: Texans 28.5, Colts 16

After trading away both Josh Downs and Keenan Allen at the trade deadline last week, it makes at least some basic sense that the Colts would lean more heavily into their running game with their best offensive player, Jonathan Taylor. What nobody saw coming was how heavily Seth McGowan would factor into the equation as well.

The run game vastly overshadowed the passing attack, as Daniel Jones and the Colts had only 22 pass attempts compared to 29 called runs. Of those runs, 28 went to Taylor (16) and McGowan (12). Both averaged well over five yards per carry, with Taylor notching 83 yards and McGowan rushing for 77 yards while hitting paydirt with a touchdown in the second quarter. With the Colts trotting out what amounts to a Week 18 or even preseason lineup consisting of Laquon Treadwell, Deion Burks and Anthony Gould as their most-frequently used wide receivers, leaning much more heavily into the run will take some pressure off Jones and the receivers to make plays.

Notably, Taylor ran routes on 80% of dropbacks, in stark contrast to McGowan's 8%. This feels like an extreme version of the early-season Rams split between Kyren Williams and Blake Corum. Against a team like the Dolphins, a split like this can be successful. Against the Texans on the road in Week 11? That's another story.

Fantasy Advice: Having to split carries brings Taylor down to high-end RB2 status, while McGowan is certainly flex-worthy if this volume holds.

Warren was a trendy name in fantasy last week leading up to Week 10, primarily because Downs and Allen were gone and their departures potentially opened up a higher target share. Well… Warren caught two passes for one yard. Not good. Warren actually had three times as many rushing yards (three) on his lone carry as he had receiving yards. While this is only one week of data, Warren's fantasy production has primarily come through receptions, with a low aDOT (4.6 yards this season), rather than downfield opportunities or touchdowns. He has two touchdowns this season, with neither coming since Week 2. While Warren should have some solid games for the rest of the season, his stock in this barebones offense has to dip. It's fair to say that Warren has fallen outside the top 12 at the position.

Fantasy Advice: The Texans have been one of the better teams against tight ends in fantasy this season, allowing the sixth-fewest fantasy points to the position. Given the issues the Colts have had on offense, Warren is difficult to count on as a locked-in starter right now. He's somebody you're hoping can compile receptions in PPR leagues and grab a touchdown. That slots him in as a high-end TE2 moving forward.

We have a bona fide hot streak here from our boy Stroud, with multiple touchdown passes in each of his last four games. Stroud tossed three more touchdown passes against the Browns in Week 10, and this Texans passing attack has been excellent for most of the season. It turns out that honing in on Collins, who had a team-high 16 targets, seems to be the right move in most circumstances. However, Stroud has spread the wealth across several receivers in recent weeks. Stroud has routinely pushed the ball downfield, with 437 and 388 team air yards over the last two weeks. Both figures ranked among the top five across all teams in their respective weeks. Basically, you should be starting Stroud in most circumstances, especially with a handful of viable fantasy starters like Jordan Love and Matthew Stafford on bye.

Fantasy Advice: On a short week against the downtrodden Indianapolis Colts, Stroud is a locked-in QB1 for this game.

Montgomery is trending toward making his return for the Texans, marking his first game action since Week 5, when he injured his groin and left the game against the Titans. This is certainly notable because Woody Marks left the game in Week 10 with a concussion. With a short turnaround for Thursday Night Football against the Colts, Marks was predictably ruled out. Montgomery should be fresh and ready to go for Week 11, but what can you reasonably expect from him? Averaging just 2.7 yards per carry, Montgomery stumbled out of the gates this season from an efficiency standpoint. But volume is king, and Montgomery is very likely to get it, even with Emari Demercado behind him.

Fantasy Advice: For this matchup against the Colts, who allow the fifth-most fantasy points to opposing backfields, Montgomery is a volume-based RB2 in a good matchup. Hopefully, that volume can make up for the glaring lack of efficiency he has shown this season.

Marvin Harrison Jr. has also been ruled out, shortening the receiver rotation to just four players. Unfortunately, that hasn't helped a player like Tank Dell, who couldn't surpass 50% of snaps or 55% of routes in Week 10 after a massively productive 4-106-1 stat line in Week 9. Dell saw just four targets and finished with a 3-21-0 line, taking a backseat to Collins and Xavier Hutchinson, who had nine targets. Dell's targets were of the shorter variety as well, with a 6.8-yard aDOT.

Even without Harrison, Dell couldn't get on the field more, which could mean he is on a strict cap for snaps and routes. Perhaps Dell elevates his involvement in Week 11 against the Colts? It is worth mentioning that Stroud told reporters earlier this week that Dell needs to be more involved. No matter how you slice it, Dell is a very thin play this week, but all hands have to be on deck during a week with six teams on bye.

Fantasy Advice: I'd be wary of Dell's lack of opportunity, which could also be affected by the game script, given that the Texans are almost two-touchdown favorites at home against a punchless Colts team. Dell is only a low-end flex option, but he could be excellent for your lineup if Stroud finds him deep for a score.

Implied Point Total: Bills 31, Dolphins 14.5

Gordon has operated as Miami's workhorse since the season-ending injury to De'Von Achane at the start of the Dolphins' Week 3 game against the Chiefs. Gordon has been useful as RB19, scoring 13.5 fantasy points per game from Week 3 through Week 10. That is the same fantasy-points-per-game average as Derrick Henry and a better average than players such as Ashton Jeanty, Blake Corum and Rhamondre Stevenson. Gordon's problem is the Miami offense in general: the Dolphins just aren't scoring a ton. Not exactly a surprise or a shocker, but that's the rub with players who are productive on bad teams: you just don't get the touchdown equity or positive game scripts that allow you to run the ball when ahead. Gordon will just have to create for himself at this point because, as Billy Zane once said, "A real man creates his own luck."

Fantasy Advice: While far from dominant, the Bills' run defense has not been the sieve it has been in recent years. Although Buffalo has surrendered a fair amount of production to the position (122.4 total yards per game), no individual runner has surpassed 60 rushing yards against them in 2026. Pair that with the likelihood that Buffalo jumps out to a two-score lead in the first half, and it is difficult to envision a scenario in which Gordon is much more than a high-end flex option in Week 11.

Dare I say it? We might have a fantasy-viable Miami Dolphins wide receiver in our midst. The kids are saying that Douglas is mogging the rest of the receivers in Miami with his target-maxxing.

While that last sentence was incredibly brutal not only to read but also to type, Douglas has been anything but over the last few weeks. Douglas has at least seven targets in each game and double-digit fantasy points in three of his last four games. While the Dolphins' offense is certainly volatile with Kyle McCord or, frankly, anybody under center, McCord is at least a pass-first quarterback who ended up with 32 pass attempts in Week 10, the third-highest total of the season for the Dolphins. We're not going overboard with Douglas here, but he has stepped up above all the other options in Miami. So, if you're in need, taking a shot on a rookie isn't the worst decision to make.

Fantasy Advice: With the Bills looming in Week 11, the opportunity for some garbage-time passing is very much in play. That, coupled with the Bills' deficiencies against fantasy wide receivers to begin with, makes me like Douglas even more as a solid flex option. We have Douglas as a mid-range flex play against the Bills in Week 11, but he has been solidly productive over the last few weeks and has become somewhat viable with a six-team bye week coming up.

Must-Start Players: QB Josh Allen, RB James Cook III

When the Bills get going against an undermatched opponent, you can usually bank on them being extremely run-heavy, and that's exactly what happened. Josh Allen had only 21 pass attempts against the Jets and was content to run Cook and company. That said, DJ Moore was the only Bills receiver among the seven other pass-catchers who saw a target to receive more than three targets. He ran 80% of routes and led Buffalo in everything with a 4-90 line and a touchdown at the end of the third quarter. It does feel like it's Moore and then the rest of the receivers in terms of the receiving hierarchy, which is good! But we know the Bills love nothing more than shutting down the passing game so they can run the ball down their opponents' throats, so that's always a danger. Moore tends to get his, though, so we're generally happy here.

Fantasy Advice: The Dolphins have surprisingly been one of the stingiest pass defenses in the league, although much of that has to do with the fact that they are facing a league-low 27 pass attempts per game. Receivers are seeing just over nine targets per game against Miami, making it next to impossible for one wideout, much less multiple ones, to post quality numbers. Moore is good enough to turn in a WR2-level effort, but he is a much safer bet as a WR3 this week.

Implied Point Total: Bears 30, Saints 18.5

It was a good matchup on paper for the Saints at home against the Carolina Panthers, but Shough could only muster 187 yards through the air on 35 pass attempts. He is scrambling a little more now as well, totaling 76 rushing yards in his last two games, but he has posted just one game above 20 fantasy points in his last five outings after starting the first three weeks of the season with 25.2, 22.4 and 24.8 fantasy points. Shough has been disappointing, to say the least. A newly energized Bears defense at home doesn't exactly help matters here in Week 11.

Fantasy Advice: It's hard to trust Shough as anything more than a streaming quarterback for Week 11. There is the possibility of a shootout, but this is a volatile matchup that the Bears could end up shutting down at home with their much-better defense.

Even with the return of Travis Etienne Jr., nothing much really changed. The Saints are somehow even worse on the ground than they were with Audric Estime running the ball by himself. Etienne saw the most touches (11), compared with Estime's eight and Alvin Kamara's five. It's just a nasty split that doesn't have to be this way. The hope is that Etienne will see his role expand in the coming weeks, but he was splitting time with the other members of the backfield even before he got hurt. So why would we expect that to change now? And why would the Saints even sign Etienne to a four-year, $48 million contract to use him in this way?

Fantasy Advice: This backfield is disgusting, and the only member I would roster is Etienne. Sadly, Etienne is merely a low-end desperation flex with all of these teams on bye.

After a promising first game in which Tyson earned four targets on just 16 routes (35% of dropbacks), his workload was ramped up considerably in Week 10. Tyson received a team-high 13 targets and parlayed those opportunities into 11 receptions for 80 yards. Everything over the last two games has been very short, with a 5.6-yard aDOT on his combined 17 targets, but the intent is clearly there to get him going. It did affect Chris Olave, as he earned just four targets in Week 11 against the Panthers, but everything should normalize there. Tyson went from running routes on 35% of dropbacks in Week 9 to 72% in Week 10. So, if you haven't already started putting Tyson in your lineups, it's now time to do so. We may just have a league-winning rookie receiver in our midst for the stretch run.

Fantasy Advice: With Tyson emerging for fantasy managers and a matchup against the Bears, the defensive attention Olave receives could create more fruitful opportunities for Tyson and the rest of the offense. Tyson is a low-end fantasy WR3 with plenty of room to maneuver in the coming weeks.

Must-Start Players: QB Caleb Williams, WR Luther Burden III, TE Colston Loveland

After the Bears' bye in Week 10, D'Andre Swift is likely to have a familiar backfield mate alongside him in Kyle Monangai. Monangai was a full participant on Wednesday, which suggests he is likely to return for Week 11. After missing five games, Monangai should return to double-digit carries, something he had in all four of his games this season. That doesn't mean Swift is a pushover, as Swift has excelled as RB10 with 18.1 fantasy points per game over those five weeks. Swift remains a very safe start, while Monangai makes for a very intriguing lineup pivot in the flex in a Week 11 fantasy landscape with six teams on bye.

Fantasy Advice: The Saints allow the second-most fantasy points per game to running backs, so there should be ample opportunity for both Swift and Monangai to operate in Week 11. Swift is a mid-range RB2, while Monangai is a perfectly usable flex option as long as he's active.

Leave it to me to call Odunze the "Ringo Starr" of the Bears' pass-catchers and then have him score a touchdown in Week 9 against the Buccaneers. But that's what happened. Consider my face rubbed in the dirt! However, it doesn't change the fact that Odunze has routinely fallen behind Burden and Loveland in the pecking order for Chicago this season. That said, Odunze has certainly made plays.

For our fun little game within a game that is fantasy football, it's hard to translate that into fantasy usefulness, even if Odunze scored his first touchdown of the season. He has just two games with more than six targets this season and one game with more than four receptions. Maybe I'm a hater, but I just don't think he's somebody we can put our full trust in unless there's an injury to one of the receivers ahead of him. Despite the Bears' concentration of targets among their featured players, Williams still gets the ball to a bunch of other players, like Kalif Raymond and, lately, Zavion Thomas. Any other secondary player seeing targets hurts the bottom line of the top players the most, and arguably none more than Odunze.

Fantasy Advice: With the Saints coming to Soldier Field after Chicago's bye, it's a slightly better-than-average matchup for the Saints, so we'll take the concentration at the top and just call Odunze a flex play for Week 11.

Implied Point Total: Cowboys 31.5, Titans 23

Tate hasn't played in a game since Week 7. After missing two games with a bye sandwiched in between, Tate is expected to suit up for the Titans in Week 11. A shoulder injury has kept Tate out of action, but head coach Robert Saleh stated that he's "hopeful" his first-round rookie will return to action against the Cowboys. With Wan'Dale Robinson hitting IR with an ankle injury on Tuesday, Tate's presence is needed now more than ever. However, Elic Ayomanor and Chimere Dike have been capable fill-ins for Tate and Robinson, so there could be more target competition in Week 11 and beyond than there was in Weeks 1-7. Still, Tate has been head and shoulders above the other pass-catchers for Tennessee this season.

Fantasy Advice: Keep an eye on the Titans' practice reports, but if Tate is a go for Week 11 against the Cowboys, he's a solid WR3 play with big upside in a good matchup.

Must-Start Players: QB Dak Prescott, RB Javonte Williams, WR CeeDee Lamb, WR George Pickens

After the top three Cowboys pass-catchers, no other Cowboy had more than three targets in their offensive (complementary) showcase against the 49ers, where they lost 46-40. Fantasy managers mostly don't care about points, but seeing 40 points on the ledger for the losing team more often than not means fantasy goodness. After Pickens' 18 targets and 43.5 PPR points, which paced the position, and Lamb's 7-72-1 on 13 targets, Flournoy saw a very healthy seven targets and was able to get into the end zone with a 6-71 performance.

Flournoy has run routes on 81% of dropbacks this season. While he's clearly overshadowed by Lamb and/or Pickens, Flournoy is somehow the overall WR25 and just off a 100-target pace, with very usable production in a high-volume offense. Week 11 sets up well, as the Cowboys will host a Titans offense that is finding some footing. As bad as the Cowboys' defense is, that should be a nice spot for somebody like Flournoy to capitalize on the added opportunity, with Jake Ferguson slated to miss the next handful of games with a knee injury.

Fantasy Advice: With six teams on bye, Flournoy is a solid flex option against the Titans, who allow the most fantasy points to opposing wide receivers.

You Can Order Room Service From This $29.5 Million Full-Floor Ritz-Carlton Residence on Central Park South

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A $29.5 million residence spanning an entire floor of the Ritz-Carlton Hotel has hit the market in New York City.

Measuring 5,724 square feet—which is nearly three times the median in its ZIP code—Residence 29 at 50 Central Park South is one of the city’s “most prestigious addresses,” according to listing agent Zina Raslan of the Gambino Group at Compass.

The five-bedroom, five-plus-bathroom dwelling is one of only a dozen permanent residences exclusive to the hotel. And while it’s accessible through a private elevator vestibule, it offers a unique perk that’s also available to Ritz-Carlton guests.

“You can order room service from your very own home,” Raslan confirmed.

With ample east- and west-facing windows found throughout the home, the skyline and Central Park views are absolutely spectacular. There are even two private terraces that look over the park—one for dining and one for lounging.

“These spaces give you a reason to really pause and enjoy the setting of New York City,” Raslan said, conjuring ideas of “morning coffee overlooking the greenery of Central Park” or “dinner outside as the lights change.”

The building originally opened in 1930 as the Hotel St. Moritz, designed by Emery Roth, the architect behind The San Remo and the Beresford. In 2002, it was reimagined as the Ritz-Carlton, with an exclusive collection of private residences above the hotel.

The residence is an excellent spot for hosting, with three dining areas—a formal dining room, a casual breakfast nook, and the aforementioned outdoor space—all connected by a seamless flow from the gorgeous Calacatta marble kitchen.

A unique solarium serves as a light-flooded space to relax, separate from the rest of the home.

“What’s really rare about this residence is that its layout has been thoughtfully divided into two separate bedroom wings,” explained Raslan.

The one wing has two bedrooms, and the other is home to the primary suite and two additional bedrooms.

“It creates a really nice sense of privacy and separation,” she added. “Whether it’s your family staying here or hosting guests, it’s the perfect way to lay it out.”

The sprawling primary suite—which Raslan accurately described as of “the moment”—is a true retreat from the bustle of the city, which you’d barely notice is just steps away if not for the sweeping city views.

The bedroom is accompanied by a “massive primary suite” with a double sink and more stunning Calacatta marble. There are two added spaces in the suite. The first is a “zen and serene” private dressing room with artfully designed wardrobes and a coordinating vanity space, while the second is an office.

The bedroom also provides access to one of the home’s terraces.

“You are truly in the heart of New York City. You have Central Park at your doorstep, Fifth Avenue moments away, and everything that makes New York extraordinary around you,” Raslan said.

“Yet here, on the 29th floor, you get this sense of calm and privacy, and that’s really what makes residence 29 so special to me. An entire floor to call your own, two private terraces overlooking the park, and a home that’s ready to move into.”

Hannah Chambers is a senior celebrity and lifestyle reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2026. She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

Golden Boy 2026: Honest Ahanor voted best Italian player of the year

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Honest Ahanor continues his rise. The 18-year-old defender won the Best Italian Golden Boy 2026 award, recognizing the best young Italian player of the year. The Crystal Palace resident succeeds Francesco Pio Esposito, winner of this distinction in 2025. A great recognition for the man who quickly established himself as one of the most promising talents of his generation. Note that the Golden Boy 2026, which rewards the best U21 player playing in Europe, went to Pau Cubarsí, defender of FC Barcelona.

This distinction also crowns a particularly eventful year for Ahanor on the transfer market. After discovering the high level with Atalanta, where he played 35 matches during the 2025-2026 season, the Italian international attracted the attention of Chelsea. The Blues have formalized an agreement with the Bergamo club for a transfer estimated at €47 million, with an arrival scheduled for the summer of 2027. In the process, the defender was sent on loan to Crystal Palace for the 2026-2027 financial year in order to continue his progress in the Premier League. An already remarkable journey for the young Italian, who has just received a new individual award.

STAT+: Before midterm losses, MAHA spent on itself instead of candidates

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

By Daniel Payne, Isabella Cueto, and Chelsea Cirruzzo

Daniel covers the intersection of the health industry and the federal government. He reports on corporate influence in government, the health consequences of federal policies, and the politics of health care. Confidential tips can be sent on Signal at danielp.100.

Isabella is a chronic disease reporter writing about how the Trump administration is tackling the nation’s health epidemic — or not. You can reach her on Signal at isabellacueto.03.

Chelsea covers the Department of Health and Human Services with a particular focus on power and influence at the top. Confidential tips can be sent via Signal at chelseacirruzzo.42.

WASHINGTON — When Tony Lyons, leader of the largest Make America Healthy Again organizations in Washington, spoke to supporters the day after the election, he was sullen.

“MAHA was up against some of the most powerful and best funded interests in the world — they backed the candidates running against us,” he said on a Zoom call, books published by his company prominently arrayed behind him. “The forces that had been lined up against us are just monstrous.”

But filings show that the MAHA PAC Lyons leads, with more than $10 million to spend through the cycle, scarcely used its funds to push back on those forces. An examination by STAT found that the MAHA PAC was instead spending big on its allies, boosters, and an affiliated nonprofit.

Those investments often came at the expense of spending on political races, including for MAHA candidates in competitive districts, campaign finance experts suggested.

America is in a turkey slump

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

One of the most quintessential American things is having roasted turkey on the table at a Thanksgiving feast. While that remains the norm, Americans are eating much less of this kind of poultry today than in the past. Annual consumption has fallen by more than 25% over the past three decades, while demand for chicken has soared.

I’m a business school professor who has written about the economics of various holidays. I find turkeys fascinating, because even though I live in an urban area, a large group of wild turkeys has taken over my backyard.

And I can offer several good explanations for the turkey slump.

Receding since the late 1990s

Domesticated turkeys, unlike their wild cousins, are a big business.

More than $5 billion worth of the birds were sold in 2025. That’s no chicken feed.

However, it’s the latest sign of the industry’s decline. U.S. consumers are drifting away from eating turkey. Back in 1995, the average American consumed 17.7 pounds of turkey each year. By 2025, consumption had fallen to just 13.2 pounds.

While turkey consumption started to recede in the late 1990s, it began accelerating around 2008, when the Great Recession was underway. Occasionally, turkey production ticks upward a little, as happened in 2026 as demand for niche products like turkey jerky rose, but the long-term picture is one of steady decline.

It’s not like Americans are eating less poultry overall. Chicken consumption has steadily grown during this turkey slump, rising about 50% from 69 pounds in 1995 to 104 pounds in 2025.

In other words, we’re eating almost eight times more chicken than turkey. Well, at least the 95% of Americans who aren’t vegetarian or vegan are.

More costly at every step in the supply chain

Government agencies, researchers, and some pundits have identified two possible explanations for why Americans are eating less turkey.

First: pocketbook concerns. Turkey often costs more than chicken, except when it goes on sale just before Thanksgiving.

Over the past 25 years, wholesale turkey prices per pound averaged about 10% higher than chicken, according to U.S. Department of Agriculture data. That price, which is what big retailers typically pay, affects what you owe at checkout.

For example, just before the 2026 Thanksgiving sales began, a Bell & Evans fresh whole turkey was going for $4.99 a pound, while a Bell & Evans fresh whole chicken cost $3.49 a pound at my local supermarket. That’s $1.50 per pound difference, which is significant in an economy where many people feel economically pinched.

Nationally, you will see similar price differences, with fresh turkey being more expensive than fresh chicken.

So, why is turkey pricier than chicken?

Both turkeys and chickens are fed a diet of corn and soybeans. However, turkeys consume a higher percentage of soybeans. And soybeans currently cost about twice as much as corn.

The latest data shows turkey feed costs are up almost 80%, without accounting for inflation, since 2000, while chicken feed costs have climbed only 60%.

Too big for more families

The second reason has to do with the size of today’s families.

The typical Bell & Evans whole chicken my grocery store sells weighs approximately 4 pounds, while its fresh turkeys tend to range between 10 and 12 pounds.

Big birds are handy if you have a lot of mouths to feed, but American families have shrunk over time. Today the average household includes only 2.5 people. The extra half-person does not represent children who are picky eaters or people who don’t like eating meat. It just means the typical family has between two and three people.

That means cooking a 10-pound turkey results in 4 pounds of food per person, which is far more than almost anyone who is not a competitive food eater can consume.

Cooking a 4-pound chicken, however, results in a more manageable 1.2 pounds of poultry per eater after getting rid of the bones, which make up about a quarter of a chicken. In simple terms, buying a whole chicken often results in one or two meals per person. Cooking a whole turkey often yields enough meat to produce meals for an entire week.

There are two more factors to consider. First, a 10-pound turkey costs a lot more than a 4-pound chicken. A 10-pound fresh bird at $4.99 a pound sets you back almost $50, while a $3.49-per-pound chicken that weighs 4 pounds costs under $14.

Last, bigger birds take longer to roast. Cooking a 10-pound turkey in an oven takes about three hours, while a 4-pound chicken takes about two. That extra hour is a big burden in an increasingly time-pressed society.

In case you were wondering, the world record for eating a turkey is held by Joey Chestnut. He gobbled down 9.35 pounds of turkey off the bone in 10 minutes in 2014. Please do not try that at home.

What about wild turkeys?

If you need to save money on turkey this holiday season, you should consider buying a frozen one. They are much cheaper.

But can you save even more by going turkey hunting?

Given all the large wild turkeys roosting in my backyard, I looked into what it would take. I live in Massachusetts, where hunting wild turkeys before Thanksgiving is legal.

For much of the fall, hunters are allowed to use only bows and arrows. Beyond needing to be a proficient archer, they also have to purchase a sporting license, a turkey permit, and an archery stamp for $95 each year.

Having learned about that, I’m sticking to buying my turkeys at the supermarket.

Jay L. Zagorsky is an associate professor of business at Boston University.

What's New in PHP 8.6

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

PHP 8.6 is a major update of the PHP language, with new features including Partial Function Application, a new Duration class, a Polling API, and a new clamp() function

PHP 8.6 has not been released yet. It will be released on November 19, 2026.

More expressive code, stronger types, and scalable I/O.

Allows some of a function's arguments to be provided in advance, returning a Closure that accepts the rest.

The Duration class represents elapsed time as measured by a stopwatch.

Scalable I/O multiplexing using epoll, kqueue, and other native mechanisms, available to event loop libraries like ReactPHP and AMPHP.

Ensures that a given value (numeric or otherwise) falls within a specified range.

A standard, type-safe way to express sort order that can be shared across libraries.

Allows readonly properties to declare default values, so fixed values no longer have to be assigned in the constructor.

Allows some of a function's arguments to be provided in advance, using ? and ... placeholders for the rest. The result is a Closure, and the function is only called once the remaining arguments are passed to it.

The Duration class represents elapsed time as measured by a stopwatch. Its representation is independent of any time and timezone and can represent a range of more than 250 years with nanosecond precision. This makes it well-suited to represent timeouts or benchmark timings in an expressive way.

The new Io\Poll API efficiently monitors many streams at once using the best mechanism available on each platform (epoll, kqueue, event ports, or WSAPoll), without the scalability limits of stream_select(). It is designed as a common foundation that event loop libraries such as ReactPHP and AMPHP can build on.

Stream operations can now be configured to throw a StreamException or to fail silently instead of emitting warnings. Errors are reported as structured StreamError objects with a StreamErrorCode enum case, the wrapper name, and the affected path or URL, and can also be retrieved later with stream_last_errors() or passed to a custom error handler.

Ensures that a given value (numeric or otherwise) falls within a specified range. Otherwise, the nearest boundary value is returned.

The SortDirection enum provides a shared, type-safe way to express sort order with its Ascending and Descending cases. Previously, every library had to define its own way of representing sort direction, resulting in a mix of incompatible integers, strings, booleans, and custom enums. Since SortDirection is built into PHP, libraries can accept it in their public APIs without needing adapter code.

Learn more about this feature in The PHP Foundation’s blog.

Function and method parameters can now have their own DocComments, which are available via the new ReflectionParameter::getDocComment() method. This allows each parameter to be documented right next to its declaration, without repeating its name and type in a @param tag.

With readonly properties, asymmetric visibility, and property hooks, checking whether a property is public is no longer enough to know whether it can be read or written. The new methods answer this directly for a given scope, and optionally for a specific object, taking into account visibility, initialization state, hooks, and magic methods.

Instance readonly properties, including those in readonly classes, can now declare a default value. This makes it simple to implement get-only interface properties with fixed values, which previously required assigning them in a constructor. The default counts as the property's initialization, so the constructor cannot overwrite it.

PHP 8.6 has not been released yet. It will be released on November 19, 2026.

The full list of changes is recorded in the ChangeLog.

Please consult the migration guide for a detailed list of new features and backward-incompatible changes.

PHP is developed and maintained by a global community of contributors. The PHP Foundation helps fund this work. If PHP is part of your business or projects, consider sponsoring the Foundation.

Barcelona sporting director Deco has left the door wide open for Liverpool to sign Ronald Araujo permanently.

Reports from Spain in recent days have indicated that Merseyside chiefs are keen to take up the option to sign the defender outright for £47m, with the player also understood to be ready to stay in L4 beyond next summer.

Liverpool’s chances of securing him on a permanent basis would appear to have been greatly boosted by comments from Deco.

Right now, though, there’s no excuse for Anfield chiefs not to go all-out to secure a permanent transfer, especially with Deco indicating that Barcelona would be prepared to sanction his sale. Don’t fumble this one please, Julian Ward!

Iran relies on China and Russia as US pressure grows, but both powers are pursuing broader interests

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As Donald Trump seeks to tighten economic pressure on Iran, China remains its biggest oil customer and Russia a key partner. But both powers are balancing support for Tehran against broader interests with Washington.

As Donald Trump, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin meet in Shenzhen, Washington wants to tighten its blockade of Iran, but China remains Tehran's main oil buyer and Russia offers it political and logistical support.

And when Trump, Xi and Putin sit down together, Iran will not be at the table, even though Washington's pressure campaign against Tehran is expected to be among the issues discussed.

For Tehran, relations with both China and Russia are routinely described as strategic. That relationship looks more complicated from Beijing and Moscow.

Both countries oppose Washington's efforts to isolate Iran and maintain political and economic ties with Tehran. But both have broader interests in their relationships with the United States and may see Iran partly through the prism of their own negotiations with Washington.

That raises a central question: Can Trump's strategy of economically isolating Iran succeed if China and Russia refuse to fully cooperate?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

China is currently the most important destination for Iranian oil.

In 2025, it was widely reported that Chinese buyers took more than 80% of Iran's seaborne crude exports, much of it through independent refiners and at steep discount because of sanctions.

And as China provided Tehran an economic lifeline, Iran also became increasingly dependent on Chinese manufactured goods, machinery and industrial inputs.

Mohsen Solhdoost, an assistant professor of international relations at Xi'an Jiaotong-Liverpool University, says China is important to Washington's strategy but not decisive on its own.

"If the objective is to reduce Iranian export earnings, restrict access to equipment and make economic and military recovery more difficult, the United States can achieve substantial results without Chinese cooperation," he told DW.

But forcing Iran to accept Washington's preferred nuclear or regional policies is another matter.

"The ability to interrupt trade is much easier to demonstrate than the ability to determine another government's political choices," Solhdoost said.

That distinction has become more important as US sanctions enforcement and maritime blockade efforts have increased pressure on Iranian crude oil exports.

In November, Washington announced Task Force Iron Gate, an initiative aimed at disrupting the movement of sanctioned goods, military equipment and revenue through Iran's land borders and the Caspian Sea.

Even so, these trade links have not disappeared. The commercial and security interests of countries including Iraq, Pakistan and Turkey mean that goods continue to move across the region, albeit under greater scrutiny.

As a result, China's role in Iran's economy remains significant, but Solhdoost argues that demand alone cannot sustain Iranian exports. The oil must be able to reach international markets.

"A Chinese buyer cannot keep Iranian export revenues flowing simply by remaining willing to buy if newly loaded tankers cannot reach the market," Solhdoost said.

This economic pressure is shaping Tehran's financial planning.

A senior official at Iran's Plan and Budget Organization recently said the government was preparing its 2027-2028 budget under several scenarios, including one in which oil revenue falls to zero.

That does not mean Iran has adopted a zero-oil budget, but considering such a scenario illustrates how seriously Tehran views the situation.

At the same time, Chinese trade can preserve critical parts of the Iranian economy even if overall commerce shrinks.

Machinery, electronics, spare parts and industrial components can still reach Iran by rail and road. Such trade cannot replace maritime commerce at comparable scale or cost, but it can keep selected factories and infrastructure operating.

Such Chinese support could help Iran endure pressure without preventing economic deterioration, says Solhdoost.

But even for Beijing supporting Iran economically does not mean supporting every Iranian policy.

Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi has maintained support for cooperation with Tehran while calling for negotiations, safe passage through the Strait of Hormuz and stable energy supplies.

A prolonged conflict around Hormuz threatens wider energy supplies and global shipping. China therefore benefits from continued access to Iranian oil but also from regional stability.

Russia creates another obstacle to Iran's isolation. The two countries share access to the Caspian Sea and have expanded military, transport and economic cooperation.

Iranian officials have discussed ways of using Russian and northern trade routes to reduce the impact of American restrictions. There has also been speculation about oil swaps and deeper Russian assistance, although these claims remain difficult to verify.

Ahmad Vakhshiteh, director of the Moscow-based Center for Strategic Research on Developing Relations with the Middle East, believes the summit should be viewed within a larger attempt to manage the wars in both Ukraine and the Middle East.

"Russia, China and the United States appear to be seeking constructive dialogue and results that could shape a new security architecture in the international system," Vakhshiteh told DW.

The summit could be significant if it establishes a continuing format for dialogue rather than producing symbolic statements, Vakhshiteh says, while cautioning against expecting a dramatic settlement from a single meeting.

Solhdoost also warns against overstating Russian support for Iran.

The strategic partnership between Moscow and Tehran provides for security cooperation and efforts to counter unilateral sanctions, but it does not require Russia to enter a war on Iran's behalf.

Since Russia is deeply engaged in Ukraine it likely has its own priorities in negotiations with Washington, says Solhdoost.

"Russia would prioritize its own national interests in negotiations with the US over Ukraine and Iran," he said.

Moscow could support Iran while simultaneously using its relationship with Tehran as leverage in talks with Trump over Ukraine.

For Iran, ties with China and Russia remain essential. They provide alternative trade routes, export markets and diplomatic support that complicate Washington's efforts to isolate Tehran.

Yet neither Beijing nor Moscow views Iran solely through the lens of strategic partnership. Both weigh support for Tehran against their own economic interests, security concerns and dealings with Washington.

In the end, the summit may be less about whether China and Russia support Iran than about how much that support is worth when larger bargains are on the table.

United Airlines Coming Back To A Hub It De-Hubbed Over A Decade Ago Could Be Its Biggest Domestic Bet In Years

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Wikimedia Commons, Wirestock Creators | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

United Airlines has buoyed its presence at Cleveland Hopkins International Airport (CLE) recently. Several route additions this year have helped expand the US-based legacy carrier’s footprint in Northern Ohio to its largest in over a decade. They have also left locals with ample choice of United hubs and leisure destinations, making Cleveland an ever more important part of the airline’s network.

But there is a sense of irony in the move. United largely turned its back on the airport, unceremoniously winding down operations 12 years ago. This cut what was a major hub in itself from United’s network. So with the carrier now appearing to bet on the site once again in what looks to be a full-circle moment, the question is: What has changed?

United’s move back to Cleveland is particularly noteworthy because of its historical relationship with the airport. After a merger with Continental Airlines in 2010, Cleveland became one of nine hubs for the expanded carrier, which retained United’s name. Unfortunately for the airport, this would turn out to be just one of a host of factors that would see it fall out of favor with the new, combined entity.

Not only was Cleveland the smallest of the nine hubs, but it was also relatively close to United’s existing gateways at Chicago O'Hare International Airport (ORD) and Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD), as well as another former Continental base: Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR). While these fit nicely into the larger network and remain in use as hubs by United to this day, Cleveland failed to fill a significant role in the wider operation. Not least was Chicago—a huge airport in comparison, serving a far greater market—being only 315 miles (275 km) down the road.

Behind all of this was the fallout from the 2008 financial crisis, which had hammered profitability across the industry. After United and Continental joined forces to cut costs, the unviable, loss-making Cleveland base became an easy target in the now-unified United and Continental brand’s drive towards efficiency. This came in 2014, with its de-hubbing (which was actually the second time in its history that United moved away).

The de-hubbing did not mean United left Cleveland altogether, though. Rather, the carrier drastically scaled back on departures and rerouted connecting services elsewhere. Under plans laid out at the time, United said average daily departures would be cut by around 60%. Some 72 peak-day flights serving 20 destinations nonstop were to be retained, it added, down from 200 and 61 beforehand, respectively.

These fell further with time, too. By 2025, just 30 or so United flights were departing Cleveland daily. That is, until the carrier opted to expand its offerings from the base once again this year. Las Vegas’ Harry Reid International Airport (LAS), which United last flew to from Cleveland in 2024, would be served year-round with five flights a week starting in September, an announcement said in May. A winter service, running between December and April next year, would then see Miami International Airport (MIA) reappear on United’s list of destinations from the airport for the first time since 2014.

* Myrtle Beach: 11 flights from June 4, 2027.

** San Juan: 26 flights from December 17, 2026, to January 11, 2027.

*** Sarasota: 4 flights from March 13 to April 3, 2027.

Owing to the additions, United’s winter schedule for this year going into next is set to cover 14 nonstop routes from Cleveland. This will be the most among any of the 10 passenger airlines that regularly operate out of the airport during the period. It will also leave United flying out of Cleveland almost 40 times a day in the meantime, taking capacity of the airport to its highest in over a decade. No doubt, this is in response to strong local demand.

The carrier is betting big on Cleveland's travel market with the announcement of two new nonstop routes to Miami and Las Vegas.

As Scott C. Carr, a City of Cleveland Department of Port Control assistant director, commented on the news of United’s route additions in May.

“Strong demand for leisure travel continues to create opportunities to grow service and connect Northeast Ohio travelers to destinations they want most.”

This is by no means new, however, with the airport having enjoyed progressively stronger footfall for some time now.

In fact, the airport’s own data shows a clear trend of improving traffic in almost every year since United’s large-scale departure in 2014, aside from an obvious slump during the pandemic. Though passenger numbers naturally dipped as United slashed services, it only took a few years for other airlines to move in, pick up the slack, and push the figures higher once more.

Among the flurry of airlines to begin or expand operations in response were low-cost carriers Frontier Airlines and Spirit Airlines, as well as close United rivals Delta Air Lines and American Airlines, to name a few. Simply put, demand was always there, but capturing it without losing money required a different approach than the one United had committed to thus far.

Prior to 2014, Continental — and later United following their merger — primarily used Cleveland as any other airline would: to funnel passengers originating elsewhere onto flights headed elsewhere. But such an operation is complex and can come with caveats.

A major consideration is whether what are known as banked connections can be sustained. Here, airlines effectively sort their arrivals and departures into waves so that passengers have time to disembark one flight and board another. The downside is that all aircraft involved have to remain grounded for a period to give travelers time to get through the airport, which can be costly for a carrier. Alongside this, airport infrastructure and ground crews must be in place to support sudden spikes in activity as planes come and go in waves.

Clearly, the practice was ultimately deemed unsustainable at Cleveland by United post-consolidation with Continental. Instead, a scaled-back, arguably simpler operation targeting those originating in or traveling to the area has taken its place. The bonus for United is that the fewer moving parts in a point-to-point system, compared to a hub-and-spoke model, make reacting to demand simpler — especially when the personnel and infrastructure to accommodatechanges are already in place.

Newark is the main United hub, while it has other major hubs in other airports across the country.

Indeed, United drastically cut back on services from Cleveland, but it did not fully scrap them. In fact, the airline remained its largest by passenger traffic, which gives some context into the weight of the de-hubbing decision on the airport. Either way, that meant United retained everything it needed to fly from the site — including a pilot base, cabin crew domicile, and maintenance facility.

But despite having the means in place to easily expand its schedule from Cleveland, United has found itself in a far less dominant position there today. Yes, it is still the airport’s primary carrier, but that is by no means as clear-cut as before the de-hubbing.

United accounted for a 28.84% share of Cleveland’s passengers over the year to August 2013, according to the US Bureau of Transportation Statistics. ExpressJet was the airport’s next-largest operator at the time, with a 14.60% share. Come the 12 months to August this year, United’s share was a more moderate 23.77%, while the gap to the second-largest - now Frontier with 21.47% - had narrowed drastically.

In other words, competition has heated up, and a string of nonstop routes passengers can choose from in Cleveland are serviced by more than one carrier — hence the risk United has had to accept in moving back in. But another factor may have contributed to the decision.

Spirit took the axe to its network prior to its collapse this year. This involved closing its base in Cleveland, where it had historically held a near 10% market share and from which it had even flown to the likes of Miami and Las Vegas. How much that has influenced United’s decision-making is debatable. Regardless, it has no doubt left a gap to be filled.

"Council of ministers meeting since morning, extensive discussions on good governance initiatives," he wrote on social media.

Eleven secretaries, including those from the Home and Finance ministries, made presentations, according to local media. Topics included a recent World Bank report on India's progress in reducing extreme poverty, governance reforms, and welfare and youth schemes.

Modi sought feedback on targets set at the ministers' September "chintan shivir," or "brainstorming camp," where ministries were told to ensure 100% saturation of welfare schemes, Business Standard reported.

Jasprit Bumrah, playing his first Test in about a year, bowled New Zealand captain Tom Latham with the third ball of the match. He finished with 3-52 from 17 overs, adding Daryl Mitchell and Tom Blundell.

Nicholls and Glenn Phillips then added an unbroken 173 after lunch as the pitch eased and India's other pacers lost their lines. Nicholls' 12th Test hundred came from 188 balls, 141 of them dot balls.

Devdutt Padikkal dropped him on 102. Phillips was 94 not out.

"You play at a venue like this and against a team like India, it's a special occasion," AFP cited Nicholls as saying.
