Parts of Bangladesh shut down Thursday as police reported two members of the ruling Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP) were killed and the ancestral home of former interim leader Muhammad Yunus was set on fire.

The unrest comes ahead of former Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina's planned return from exile in India next month, despite facing the death sentence there.

Her party, the Awami League, which was banned after Hasina was ousted, called for the nationwide "lockdown" after a week of protests over the government's rejection of her request for a new passport and identity card.

Police said two local BNP leaders were killed in Narail when Awami League activists attacked a BNP march, local media reported. It added that two other people, a local BNP leader and a transport worker, died in Faridpur and Pirojpur earlier this week.

Newspaper reports said suspected BNP activists attacked or set fire to the homes of two Awami League leaders in Dhaka.

Crude bombs went off in Dhaka before dawn and at least four people were wounded, the Press Trust of India reported. Reuters said 97 platoons of the Border Guard had been deployed across seven districts, including Gopalganj and Faridpur.

Authorities said flammable material was probably thrown into an unused ground-floor room of Yunus's Chattogram family home, and that relatives living upstairs were safe. At least 37 Grameen Bank branches and offices, which Yunus founded, have been targeted since November 10, the bank said.

Home Minister Salahuddin Ahmed has said those behind the violence will be identified and prosecuted, and he accused Awami League leaders abroad of inciting it. Awami League leaders could not be reached for comment, Reuters said.

“If there's no one else to do it, he should come out and do it himself. Or is there nothing to say? I'd suggest that there isn't anything to say, otherwise he'd have come out and said it. But come out and say it if there's nothing to say. At least do that.”

And Vessey, who has been captain and manager at Crawley, agreed. “It’s been four weeks of silence, pretty much. Just be honest. People just want you to be honest with them.

Say something like ‘we're trying to recruit because of the position we're in, we're finding it a bit difficult’. ‘We're relying on Kevin Blackwell to hopefully improve that position so that we can get a permanent appointment’. I think most people would accept that. You own the club.

“You've taken on that responsibility of guiding it. So guide it. And in any business, communication is key. So football's a business. You want your supporters to turn up week in, week out, then communicate with them. And I think that's an imperative for anybody.”

Vessey added: “If he's a businessman,then communication is key in most businesses. So he should understand that keeping everybody on side is the way forward, not to be completely silent.”

Khalili took over leadership of the club in August 2025 as KB Sports and Leisure but was previously a minority shareholder when WAGMI were in charge.

Trump says U.S., China, Russia ‘uniquely positioned’ to discuss reducing nuclear weapons at historic meeting

📅 2026-11-19  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump said the U.S., Russia and China are "uniquely positioned" to reduce the world's nuclear weapons stockpile during his historic trilateral meeting with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin on Thursday.

"As the world's three largest nuclear powers by far, the United States, Russia and China are uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on earth and also to lessen the risk that those weapons will ever be used," Trump said in opening remarks.

"The power is unbelievable, unthinkable," he said, adding that it's "a very hard thing" for countries to reduce nuclear weapons.

That would include not letting Iran have nuclear weapons, Trump said.

He said the three leaders would also discuss the war between Russia and Ukraine, and criticized "rogue globalist organizations," including the International Criminal Court.

The closely watched meeting kicked off late Thursday afternoon, following a working lunch between Trump and Xi that started at least an hour later than originally planned.

Trump told Xi and Putin they were "really making history" with the first-ever trilateral meeting between the leaders of the three countries.

He said it was an "honor" to be at the meeting with Putin and Xi, whom he congratulated on a very successful APEC summit."

"Too often past leaders have avoided talking to nations with whom they had disagreements," Trump added. "We feel the opposite."

At the trilateral, Xi said that for large nations, having good interactions is not a choice but a necessity, according to a translation by state media. In the report, Xi noted China's partnership with Russia, work towards constructive strategic stability in U.S. relations, and called on the three countries to respond to the world's challenges.

Xi laid out four "correct" ways for large countries to interact: mutual respect, peaceful coexistence, win-win cooperation, and adherence to dialogue, according to state media.

The Chinese president echoed Trump's comments on nuclear weapons, calling for such arms to be comprehensively prohibited and destroyed, adding that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.

Putin said Russia, China and the U.S. can strengthen global stability if they cooperate constructively, calling for the three leaders to discuss energy security and global and regional issues — including the war in Ukraine.

Beijing only confirmed the closely watched meeting on Monday. The three leaders had briefly gathered Wednesday evening as part of a welcome banquet for the APEC leaders more broadly.

iFixit Says Apple’s HomeView Breaks Easily. Here’s Why I’m Not Worried

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

iFixit found the HomeView nearly impossible to repair yourself, but smart displays don't face the same daily risks as iPhones.

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

For the past few years, Apple was looking almost DIY-friendly. The company had been moving toward smart speakers that you could take apart without damaging and iPads with accessible batteries. But the new HomeView smart display broke apart when iFixit tried to access components in Tuesday’s full analysis.

The good news? I don’t think it really matters, at least not for this particular Apple device. Here’s why.

DIY repairs are a big deal for Apple products, not just because they’re difficult, but because many people believe they have a right to repair their own phones or computers.

Making repairs more accessible also sustains a secondary market of iPhone and Mac repair services that Apple would rather tightly control — or squash entirely. That’s one reason the EU has required Apple to make iPhone batteries replaceable in 2027.

The HomeView moves sharply in the opposite direction, held together by the toughest adhesive iFixit has ever encountered in an Apple product. The glue is so stubborn that the team accidentally snapped a speaker in half during disassembly, and the logic board was bound too dangerously tight to the aluminum chassis to remove.

“Obviously, when you buy a product you don’t anticipate the accidents, and there’s no way of knowing when wear and tear will become a problem,” iFixit said in its report. “But you can be sure of one thing: The HomeView will not be an easy or pleasant repair when that time comes.”

A representative from Apple did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

iFixit also pointed out that if a HomeView device falls out of warranty, the repair typically costs as much as the $299 smart display itself.

I’m just not sure any potential HomeView users need to worry about the device’s repairability the way they would about an iPhone or Mac.

Smart displays don’t have the same DIY repair needs as the phone in your pocket. There’s no battery to replace, which is one of the most common reasons to open up an iPhone or MacBook. And while a shattered iPhone screen can easily be swapped out for a third-party replacement, no aftermarket alternatives exist for the HomeView’s custom 7-inch display. Since you can only get a replacement screen through Apple anyway, making the device easier to open wouldn’t actually help you fix it yourself.

Plus, smart displays don’t face the same environmental risks as phones and laptops, which are constantly on the move, encounter dust in pockets, accidentally dropped and exposed to potential heat and rain. The HomeView is designed to remain stationary in your home. The need for DIY repairs is simply far less of a concern.

I don’t mean to say iFixit’s teardown was unnecessary. We learned valuable information, including how the speakers and their connectors broke in several ways when iFixit tried to remove them. (The speaker assembly is actually one HomeView component I worry people may need to replace.)

“Embedded speakers are a relatively common source of failure. The under-screen speakers inside the HomeView in particular, more closely resemble laptop speakers than HomePod tweeters,” the iFixit report notes. “As far as laptop and MacBook speakers go, we’ve seen plenty of examples of damage caused by accidents and wear and tear.”

I’ve been testing smart displays and smart speakers since they first hit the market, and none of the devices I’ve tested have developed a blown or faulty speaker. In the HomePod subreddit, I couldn’t find any reports of speaker failures except from people already Frankensteining their Apple speakers with third-party components.

I wouldn’t let the fear of repair difficulties get in the way of buying a HomeView. A bigger concern is the HomeView’s compact 7-inch screen, which feels cramped for watching video, especially compared with the larger displays Amazon has rolled out across its Echo Show lineup.

Avoiding Colombia’s Escalation Trap

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ELIZABETH DICKINSON is Deputy Director for Latin America at International Crisis Group.

How a U.S.-Backed Offensive Could Help Bogotá Reclaim Its Conflict Zones

On September 21, José Luis Pérez Villanueva, better known as “El Cholo”—the second-in-command and financial architect of the criminal group Autodefensas Conquistadores de la Sierra Nevada—was killed during a Colombian police operation on the rural outskirts of Santa Marta. Within hours, militants from that organization launched a dramatic campaign of terror. The group, known colloquially as Los Pachenca, demanded that businesses across the city shutter. Some 90 percent of shops did, as videos circulated of gunmen shooting up storefronts that stayed open.

For two decades, Los Pachenca has been the unquestioned criminal authority in the beachfront city and the adjacent Sierra Nevada mountain range, charging extortion payments from businesses and residents and controlling the flow of drugs out of the Port of Santa Marta, en route to Europe and beyond. But the level of control the group exhibited during the strike was particularly jarring, given that Santa Marta is the vacation home of many of the country’s elite and a common destination for international visitors. Colombia’s government responded by militarizing the city, sending in hundreds of soldiers and police officers, who rolled into town on the main highway with tanks and air support. Many shops still stayed closed until the morning of September 25, when the group announced that it was ending the strike. For locals, Los Pachenca’s reaction was a message about who remains in charge.

Calm has since returned to Santa Marta, but the incident may be indicative of risks to come. Colombia’s newly elected president, Abelardo de la Espriella, has promised a steep military escalation against illegal groups, which have grown precipitously in recent years. As he does so, armed and criminal organizations will undoubtedly respond. Often, their target will not be the state. Instead, they will likely direct their violence against the civilian populations, whom they have spent years coopting and coercing. That is because armed and criminal groups across the country have learned that their true leverage is their ability to govern local communities—and to intimidate and punish them into silence. Even when they do attack state forces, their use of weapons such as improvised explosives and makeshift drones means that bystanders often suffer. In one recent incident in the Pacific coast department of Valle del Cauca, four drone attacks launched by an armed group in a single day left five civilians injured.

These dangers are particularly acute as de la Espriella’s pledge of a zero-tolerance crackdown against armed and criminal groups has won enthusiastic support from Washington. Both countries want big and visible victories against these outfits. They triumphantly announced a new era of cooperation when the president took office in August, including a promise of upgraded military ties. (The United States has not yet publicly pledged new weapons, but it is already providing greater intelligence and logistical assistance on some operations.) After two years of tensions and threats between President Donald Trump and leftist former President Gustavo Petro, the political realignment means that Colombia is once again the United States’ closest defense ally in South America. This is likely to continue, despite major Democratic wins in the U.S. Congress, as the relationship’s focus on security has a long history of bipartisan support.

This cooperation, and de la Espriella’s crackdown, will likely yield initial results. The Colombian government has said that it aims to use its resources to ramp up attacks against armed and criminal groups and to go after leaders who enjoyed protection as counterparts in peace talks during the past administration. Yet Colombia’s true challenge is not how to carry out attacks but what to do afterward. Surgical strikes, which see the military enter and exit quickly, leave civilians more vulnerable in the aftermath, as armed groups retaliate against alleged informants and tighten their grip in areas they control. Bogotá must ensure that every operation includes contingencies to anticipate criminal groups’ reactions and build on successful offensives by weakening their grasp on communities. The government will need to strengthen the police’s ability to patrol contested areas, a costly but necessary process that the United States and other partners should support.

The stakes are high. Bogotá has a chance to leverage its increased military pressure to open the door for the state, rewriting the story of who governs conflict areas. If the government misses this opportunity to consolidate state control, however, Colombia risks entering an escalatory spiral in which civilians pay a mounting price for a campaign intended to protect them.

Armed and criminal groups have spent the last decade expanding their reach across Colombia. According to the local think tank Pares, the number of municipalities with an armed group presence rose from 212 in 2018 to 518 last year—or about half of all municipalities. This deterioration reversed dramatic and hard-won improvements in security. After the historically violent decades of the 1990s and 2000s, military pressure and negotiations yielded a 2016 peace agreement demobilizing the former Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC), once the country’s dominant rebel group. Colombia’s countryside breathed a sigh of relief as the FARC pulled out of areas where it had been in control, which represented a quarter of the country’s land.

The FARC’s exit, however, did not pave the way for Colombia’s highly centralized and bureaucratic state to claim control of those vast geographies. Instead, old armed groups such as the National Liberation Army and newer ones such as the Gaitanista Army of Colombia (also known as the Gulf Clan) saw a chance to expand their access to trafficking routes, coca crops, illegal mines, and, ultimately, vulnerable rural populations. Kidnapping for extortion and mass displacement began to resurface, shattering optimism that progress would move in one direction. Over the last four years, membership in illegal groups has nearly doubled, to some 27,000 fighters today.

As a candidate, de la Espriella successfully harnessed public frustration by promising mano dura, or iron-fisted tactics, including aerial attacks and increased incarceration, facilitated by the construction of mega-prisons. He blamed his predecessor’s administration for accelerating insecurity. The first leftist to be elected president in modern Colombia, Petro had entered office in 2022 vowing to de-emphasize the military’s role and instead hold peace talks with various armed organizations. But not every group participated, and many of the ones that did sought to take advantage of cease-fires to cement their control over communities. Midway through his term, Petro pivoted back toward deploying the armed forces. But much of the harm was already done.

Colombia’s new president has been in office only a little more than 100 days, but the shift is marked. De la Espriella carried out three bombing raids in his first weeks. He has extradited dozens of individuals to the United States, including senior leaders, and the administration has killed or captured many others. The president is doggedly pushing to restart aerial fumigation of coca crops, which the state carried out in the early 2000s to try to reduce the number of hectares under cultivation; the practice has since been heavily restricted by court rulings over concerns about health and environmental consequences. Both in private and in public, military leaders laud the government’s new support for their operations.

The Trump administration has also welcomed these developments, which align with a regional trend favoring Washington’s approach on counternarcotics. For Colombia, the new era of security cooperation with the United States represents a return to the status quo ante of the last quarter century, which was briefly disrupted by the Petro administration. U.S.-Colombian military ties were forged through antidrug efforts and deepened in 1999 with Plan Colombia, a decadelong $10 billion aid package that funded, among other things, the expansion and professionalization of the security forces and fumigation. The Colombian police and military are deeply integrated with their American counterparts. They use U.S.- and NATO-compatible systems, travel by the hundreds to the United States each year for instruction, and receive American intelligence. In nearly every operation, at least one step (and often far more) in the logistical chain is either coordinated with Washington or relies on U.S. support. That relationship never fully stopped under Petro, but the two sides were notably wary of each other. Now bilateral trust appears to be restored.

Even before the elections, de la Espriella impressed U.S. officials by outlining his plans to reclaim security momentum for the state. The Colombian president is now acting on several explicit U.S. priorities, including extraditions and legislation to designate numerous groups as terrorist organizations. Meanwhile, Bogotá’s coca eradication targets are even more ambitious than Washington’s. At a personal level, relations are strong, too. Two of Colombia’s top military officials previously served as envoys to U.S. Southern Command.

Bogotá says that it intends to deliver results for Washington’s antidrug goals and has explicitly asked for additional support in the form of funding, aircraft, surveillance technologies, and maintenance. Although Colombia’s military remains among the best trained in the region, its logistical needs are critical. Only 13 of Colombia’s 53 Black Hawk helicopters are in working order, and defense spending has shrunk as a share of the national government’s budget, from 22.4 percent in 2003 to just 14.7 percent in 2025, with a growing proportion of those funds spent on salaries and pensions.

How much the United States will provide remains to be seen. In August, U.S. Southern Command’s General Francis Donovan discussed ways to shore up the deteriorating air fleet during a visit to Bogotá. The United States has also redeployed surveillance drones from Africa to Colombia and Ecuador, where they are already aiding intelligence collection. But Secretary of State Marco Rubio was reluctant to pledge specific aid while in Colombia in September, and most of this assistance is likely to be in kind or to stay in the hands of the U.S. military and contractors, with little entering the Colombian budget. Much of it appears to be focused on short-term offensive wins.

Colombia has returned to tactics such as aerial bombardment because they arguably worked against the FARC. Founded as a Marxist rebellion in the 1960s, the group behaved like a conventional army, dressed in uniforms, and sought out combat with the state. This meant that the Colombian military could bomb encampments and that capturing leaders left the organization at times rudderless. Slowly chipping away at the FARC’s hierarchy and control helped push the group to finally negotiate its disarmament, beginning in 2012.

But today, those same strategies are yielding diminishing returns. The current generation of armed and criminal groups has little interest in confronting the state. Instead, they behave like businesses, seeking to safeguard access to natural resources, supply chains, and trafficking routes. They live within the civilian population and rarely wear uniforms. Leaders are easily replaced and hierarchies are fluid. Critically, Colombia’s armed groups have learned that wars with the military are costly but that controlling local populations—through intimidation and violence, but also by offering economic incentives, jobs, and services—is comparatively cheap. The battlefield has evolved from a conventional conflict to a contest over who can govern territories and the people living there.

Santa Marta offers an example of how this new model of warfare works. On most days, an outsider in the city would never perceive the presence of Los Pachenca. The group’s members do not patrol or identify themselves. Yet residents know them well from the weekly or monthly extortion payments they are forced to make. Local companies and government officials essentially view the group as an inescapable reality. Pushing back carries a violent price, but paying a protection tax, taking a bribe, or facilitating illegal commerce keeps business running smoothly.

The criminal governance of rural areas is even more suffocating. Groups that emerged from splinters of the former FARC, known as dissidents, often force local residents to hold identity cards, issued so that militants have a full census of who lives in the area and can detect possible enemy fighters. In the Amazon region, these groups keep a land registry more complete than that of the state so that they know exactly whom and how much to extort.

With this intense surveillance over society, armed and criminal groups frequently deploy the civilian population as human shields against military offensives and police patrols. The most egregious example is the widespread use of forcibly recruited children as bodyguards for senior commanders. De la Espriella’s initial bombardments, which killed at least six children, were even temporarily halted by a court over concerns about minors. Senior officers admit that these types of casualties are inevitable around high-value targets, and that the groups know that the military is far less likely to proceed if it means killing children.

Armed and criminal groups will also try to use the military operations to their political advantage. When children are killed in bombardments, for example, FARC dissidents spin the local narrative into a story about how the state cares little for rural populations. The argument sticks because there is rarely a credible state presence that can serve and protect these same communities and earn their trust.

There are more immediate consequences, as well. In the aftermath of bombardments and high-level captures, armed and criminal groups often undertake what amounts to a purge of the population, killing or exiling anyone they believe to have collaborated with the state. And when the state takes out a leader, intra-group violence frequently ensues as former associates compete to take his place. Many residents shelter inside to avoid being caught up in the fighting, leaving children without school and parents without income. If new splinter groups emerge, the combat for control over neighborhoods can place even more civilians in the crossfire. This fallout widens the gap between immediate tactical results and long-term security gains. Taking out a senior leader wins applause, but conditions may well worsen on the ground.

De la Espriella’s administration has rightly identified winning territorial control, permanently dislodging criminal groups, and restoring state governance as its ultimate goals. The dilemma it now faces—exacerbated by U.S. support that focuses on attention-grabbing wins—is how to both achieve short-term results and ensure that these do not unleash new waves of violence, retaliation against civilians, and, in turn, criminal consolidation.

The challenge is twofold. First, the military needs to anticipate groups’ violent reactions and to safeguard the population. In the case of Santa Marta, for example, the government could have increased police presence in the city in the hours after El Cholo was killed. Before the next bombardment or high-level capture, the state should ensure that a ground team of soldiers or military police is ready to hold the ground, protect the community, and prevent the armed group from swiftly stepping back in to reclaim territory.

The second challenge—establishing lasting territorial control—is trickier. A permanent rural security presence might offer the best chance for Colombia’s government to change the direction of the conflict. The country’s military leadership controls only the tip of the spear, not the rest of the state. Time and again, governments “send only the military to the hardest areas,” a retired senior officer told Crisis Group. Moreover, soldiers are operating under heavy pressure for quick wins, which are measured in the number of combatants and especially high-value targets that are killed or captured. There is a real risk that by focusing largely on these statistics, the state will overlook more comprehensive policies necessary to achieve durable security.

Pressure to deliver fast results has in the past incentivized unacceptable practices. In the early 2000s, intense political demands for combat kills fueled elaborate schemes in which soldiers murdered thousands of civilians and counted them as guerrilla fatalities, a scandal known as “false positives.” These crimes deeply compromised public trust in the security forces, and the military has worked for years to build a culture and procedures that ensure they do not happen again. Today, the country’s senior military leadership is likely the first and best brake on any attempts to use excessive force. Commanders say that they are cognizant of the need to guard against rules being slowly stretched until they are broken. “We need a stronger security approach, but it also worries us when we hear there is pressure for ‘results,’” one officer said. But U.S. rhetoric and behavior, including its decision to sink what it claims are drug-smuggling boats on the high seas, compound these risks.

Success, then, may demand a strategy that is ultimately anchored in a much stronger and larger police force, fit for the job of safeguarding civilians rather than merely carrying out offensive strikes. Military operations can generate a window of opportunity by shuffling dynamics on the ground or removing certain dangerous players. Yet those reconfigurations will turn out in the state’s favor only if the resulting vacuum is eventually filled by reliable law enforcement. Take, for example, an operation that temporarily dislodges a group from a rural hamlet. If the military acts alone, it will likely enter and leave in a matter of days or even hours, allowing the armed group to quickly return. If, however, the offensive is followed up with permanent police patrols, access to the justice system, and social interventions, criminals will have a harder time regaining control.

This, of course, is easier said than done, given the government’s resource constraints. To create effective and permanent patrols, police departments operating in the countryside need more officers and equipment to maintain a presence in the most remote communities. Colombia has roughly 180,000 uniformed police officers, a figure that, per capita, puts it on the lower end of OECD countries, many of which are far more urbanized and therefore easier to patrol. In Colombia’s conflict-affected areas, outgunned officers must often stay inside their local stations for their own safety, limiting their ability to build trust and channels of communication with the population. Other state institutions, such as the justice system, say that they cannot establish a durable foothold in villages without guarantees of protection in the medium term—exactly what rural policing should strive to provide. The U.S. State Department’s Bureau of International Narcotics and Law Enforcement Affairs has funded the building and equipping of rural police stations in the past; it should expand this work. International partners with long-standing expertise in rural policing, such as Canada and France, could provide critical support, as well.

In cities, the challenge is different. In Santa Marta, for example, the state is present, but it is weak and vulnerable to the pressure and cooptation of the same groups that it must confront. The police need both more officers and better working conditions—including salaries, benefits, access to education, and social support for families—that could help them resist bribes. Regular vetting and counterintelligence checks can help ensure that police are fighting on the side of the state and not its adversaries.

Expanding the police will be an expensive, long-term undertaking. In the interim, Colombia could prioritize more personnel for critical areas and ensure that officers have a strong grounding in community policing, allowing them to understand the most pressing risks facing civilians. That means regular outreach to communities to build trust and make certain that officers are seen as having a stake in local well-being. The United States, meanwhile, must continue to help Bogotá strengthen its police and courts with direct financial and technical assistance.

Colombia, backed by renewed U.S. support, cannot afford to repeat past mistakes or ignore how the conflict has changed. Bogotá must therefore plan for and capitalize on military pressure to do something no government in Colombia’s history has done: govern well in conflict areas.

Roborock’s $359 robot mop/vacuum combo is just $199 during Amazon’s Black Friday sale

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 19, 2026 3:55 AM EST

Cleaning is the worst, which is what makes it a great job for a robot to take over. The robot maps your floors with LiDAR and pulls a rated 10,000Pa of suction. It’s a futuristic way to pawn off your chores.

Not every floor needs an app. The Shark Navigator Lift-Away NV356E upright vacuum is $139.99 (it lists for $199.99), and its trick is in the name: the canister lifts off the base so you can carry it up the stairs or reach under furniture with the hose. Shark seals the system around a HEPA filter, and a switch stops the brushroll when you move from carpet to bare floor.

A steam-assisted carpet cleaner for pet messes

Pets and carpet eventually have a disagreement. The Bissell Revolution HydroSteam Pet carpet cleaner is built for the aftermath, and it’s $299.99 against a $399.99 list price. It has three modes: a steam pretreat for stuck-on messes, a deep-cleaning wash, and a quick-dry pass that Bissell says leaves carpet dry in about 30 minutes. An 8-foot hose and two included tools, one for upholstery and one for tough stains, handle the couch and the stairs. It runs on a 25-foot cord, which reaches across most rooms (unless you’re a fancy rich guy, in which case you’re probably not looking at deal posts).

Personal Investments • Re: Vanguard Roth IRA: Sell BND, Buy VT

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Thank you. This is helpful.

One thing I'll say is I am heavily weighted US stocks (like 90%) and actually trying to improve that. I'm still learning.

Hey all. I bought some BND in my Roth IRA, and realize that was not necessary, and it should all go into VT. I am 99% sure I can sell the BND and use those funds to buy VT (all within the Roth IRA) without triggering a tax event, but just want to double check.

If so... I also have VFIAX in my Roth IRA. Should I just swap that out for VT, too?

Thank you!
1. Not taxable
2. Yes, it's probably a good idea to not only swap BND for VT but also switch VFIAX for VT.

I say "probably" because while not holding bonds in Roth IRA should improve returns, there have been periods of time where bonds outperform stocks. So 100% stock portfolio could underperform some other portfolio with stocks AND bonds.

Also VT proably underperformed a US stock portfolio in the past because US stocks outpeformed international stocks. So in the past getting rid of VFIAX for VT would have actually underperformed.

This doesn't mean it's not good to make the switch. You can't know ahead of time what's going to out/underperform, so you invest based on your philosophy (market cap or tilt to US), amount of diversification (VT is more diversified than VFIAX by geography and number of stocks and sector weightings between US and International).

This may seem pedantic, but it's important to go in with eyes wide open and not have expectations of outperformance that may or may not materialize. VT is a simple way to invest and there's nothing wrong with that. I do.

Also one final reason to say "probably" is that without knowing the context of your entire portfolio we can't say whether it's a good idea to get rid of bonds or not. There are better places to hold them than in Roth IRA. But we don't know what other accounts you have, etc.

If you want to edit your post (don't start a new one) according to asking portfolio questions we can give more targeted advice.

Lainey Wilson Looks So Different Without Signature Bell Bottoms & Hat

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lainey Wilson looked almost unrecognizable as she stepped out at the 2026 CMAs in Nashville Nov. 18 without her signature bell bottom pants and cowboy hat.

Lainey Wilson is stepping away from bell bottom country.

The “Heart Like a Truck” singer ditched her signature flare pants and Western hat for a classic Oscar de la Renta gown at the 2026 CMA Awards.

Wilson—who is serving as host for the evening’s festivities—looked stunning in a light yellow feathered gown, which featured a short train and a halter neckline. She kept her accessories simple for the Nov. 18 ceremony at the Bridgestone Arena in Nashville, only donning earrings and a few rings to finish off her simple look. (See all the stars at the show here.)

The 34-year-old—who is also nominated for Entertainer of the Year, Female Vocalist of the Year and Single of the Year for her hit “Somewhere Over Laredo”—has made no secret of her penchant for bell bottoms and a cowboy hat over the years, explaining that she was inspired to create her signature look after arriving in Nashville.

“I get to Nashville and I realized pretty quickly that as a female in this business, you have to do something that's a little outside of the box,” she shared during an interview with the Country Music Hall of Fame and Museum in April. “I wasn't gonna get away with wearing a ball cap and skinny jeans.”

The realization made her think back to her childhood, as she remembered the first pair of bell bottoms she got at the age of 9—which was also the year she wrote her first song.

“They were blue leopard print,” she recalled. “I remember putting them on as a child and just thinking—I just felt sassy. I felt like I could do anything.”

“If I still had those pants, I'd still be trying to fit in them ‘cause I felt good,” she quipped. "I felt just like I could take on the world. It was kind of like my superhero costume.”

And that superhero costume quickly became her signature, alongside the cowgirl hat that she started wearing in the late 2010s. In fact, the look has become so associated with the country star, that fans even dress up in their own version of the outfit at her shows.

“It's cool to look out into the crowd now and see everybody in their bell bottoms and their hats,” she shared. “It feels like a little bit of a movement and it just makes me happy.”

Your Commute Costs More Than Gas – Price Every Mile, Then Cut Trips & Taxes

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

If you drive to work, price each commuting mile at the IRS business mileage rate, which counts what a car costs to own, not just gas. Pay for transit, vanpools, and parking with pretax money through a commuter benefit at work. Then drive fewer days with a carpool or a remote day or two a week.

Gas is the commuting cost you notice. Insurance, repairs, and the value your car loses with each mile never show up at the pump.

The IRS business mileage rate, 76 cents a mile, counts them along with the gas. The IRS sets it from a yearly study of what cars cost to own and drive. It’s an average, and the full cost of owning a car varies by model.

Say you drive 20 miles each way, 230 days a year, and count each mile at 76 cents. That’s 9,200 miles and almost $7,000 a year. If you ride transit, add up a year of fares and parking instead.

Next, pay what you can with pretax money. Under the IRS’s fringe benefit rules, a commuter benefit at work lets you buy transit passes and vanpool rides with up to $340 a month of pretax pay. A separate $340 a month can go to parking at or near work, or where you catch transit, a vanpool, or a carpool. That pay skips federal income tax and the 7.65% Social Security and Medicare tax, so in the 22% federal bracket each dollar of fares or parking costs about 70 cents.

Then cut the trips. Working from home two of your five days takes 40% off those 9,200 miles and brings the cost at 76 cents a mile down from almost $7,000 to about $4,200. Gas and upkeep on the skipped miles stop at once, and a car driven less lasts more years.

On the days you still go in, each of these cuts your driving.

Look in your benefits portal for a commuter benefit, along with other workplace perks you may be missing, then ask your manager about working from home a day or two a week.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

American Airlines’ Airbus A321neo Fleet Grows To 90, Expects 2 More & 1 A321XLR By Year’s End

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines has expanded its Airbus A321neo fleet to 90 aircraft, with two more standard A321neos and one A321XLR expected before the end of 2026. The latest arrival continues the carrier's fleet modernization program, in which Airbus narrowbodies play an increasingly important role in its domestic and international networks.

The latest delivery follows an unusually busy period at the end of last month, when American received four A321-family aircraft in just seven days. Those deliveries included three standard A321neos and one A321XLR, registered N311NY. With no further Boeing deliveries expected this year, Airbus remains central to American's near-term fleet renewal strategy.

American's standard A321neo fleet has reached 90 aircraft, with two more expected before the end of the year. The airline is also anticipating one additional A321XLR, extending its investment in long-range, single-aisle aircraft. If all three outstanding deliveries arrive as planned, American will finish 2026 with 92 standard A321neos, alongside its growing fleet of 12 A321XLRs.

American's third-quarter financial disclosures showed that it had 165 Airbus A320-family aircraft on order as of September 30, with seven scheduled for delivery during the final quarter. The subsequent acceleration in October, when four A321-family aircraft arrived within a week, highlighted the program's pace. American also expects another 27 A321-family deliveries in 2027, although the precise split between the standard A321neo and A321XLR has not been disclosed.

The A321XLR is particularly important to American's international expansion, allowing it to serve longer routes with fewer passengers than a widebody aircraft would typically accommodate.

Brian Znotins, American's former Senior Vice President of Network Planning, highlighted the aircraft's importance when announcing the carrier's 2027 international expansion.

"The A321XLR is transforming our international network, expanding it to offer destinations to satisfy every travel palate. Combined with our investments enhancing our onboard product and in-flight experience, travelers have more reasons to choose American when they fly around the world."

American received its first A321XLR in October 2025, becoming the first US operator of the type. Its original 50-aircraft commitment was subsequently reduced to 40, including five delivered by the end of 2025. Each aircraft features 20 Flagship Suite business class seats, 12 premium economy seats, and 123 economy seats, emphasizing premium revenue over maximum capacity.

The XLR restores some of the long-range narrowbody capability American lost when it retired its 757s in 2020. Its economics make thinner transatlantic routes viable without deploying a much larger 787 Dreamliner. The aircraft initially entered service on premium transcontinental flights, before expanding into international markets as more aircraft became available.

American's 2027 schedule demonstrates its ambitions, with four additional European routes assigned to the XLR. Philadelphia-Vienna, measuring approximately 4,321 miles (6,955 km), is particularly notable as it will become the world's longest narrowbody flight. The route will operate daily from May 6 through January 2028, while New York gains new services to Amsterdam and Nice.

13 new routes were planned but now only seven are scheduled.

The standard A321neo remains central to American's high-capacity narrowbody strategy. With its fleet now at 90 aircraft, it still has firm orders for 126 more airframes as the airline continues investing in the type to support busy domestic routes and replace older aircraft.

Configured with 196 seats, including 20 First Class and 47 Main Cabin Extra seats, American's legacy A321neos accommodate 41 more passengers than the XLR. It is particularly valuable on busy domestic routes connecting American's major hubs and supports the gradual replacement of older narrowbodies. Its versatility extends internationally, with Philadelphia-Reykjavík scheduled to launch in May 2027.

American's Boeing 737 MAX 8s complement the A321neos, offering 172 seats on routes requiring less capacity. The airline also has substantial outstanding Boeing narrowbody orders, including the larger 737 MAX 10, although deliveries of that variant are not expected until 2029 or later. With an eventual planned fleet size of more than 250 A321-family aircraft, Airbus narrowbodies will continue to play a leading role in American's domestic modernization and expanding transatlantic network.

Cam Skattebo done with backflip celebration in wake of Aidan Hutchinson injury

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

That's the message from New York Giants running back Cam Skattebo in regard to his famed touchdown celebration. Skattebo, who has never been one to shy away from contact or a moment to celebrate, saw all he needed to see when it came to Aidan Hutchinson's season-ending injury.

The Detroit Lions' star celebrated a sack of New England Patriots QB Drake Maye by jumping in the air. When Hutchinson landed, he suffered a torn ACL.

And that was enough for Skattebo to retire his backflip celebration – at least for the time being.

"It's hard to see," Skattebo told reporters, via the New York Post's Ryan Dunleavy, after Wednesday's practice. "I'm saying yeah (no more). Mentally, I'm like, 'I'm not doing that.' That ain't going to happen to me."

This isn't the first time that the running back's celebration has been a topic of discussion.

Skattebo was seen on video backflipping multiple times last offseason, despite coming off a serious season-ending ankle injury in 2025. It got to a point where new head coach, John Harbaugh, was asked for his reaction. Harbaugh joked about the situation, saying in part, "Dude, at least stick it."

The 24-year-old brought the celebration back to the field in the Giants' season opener when he scored a touchdown against the Dallas Cowboys.

At the time, he left the door open for a new celebration to emerge, but the backflip resurfaced again as recently as Week 10 against the Washington Commanders on "Thursday Night Football."

So is the backflip done? Maybe for now. Or maybe not.

Skattebo noted it could come back in a big moment, like if the Giants make the playoffs.

In other words, Skattebo and the backflip are taking a break. History says they'll be back together soon enough though.

Track One Workweek in 30-Minute Blocks – Then Cut What Doesn’t Need You

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Keep a log of one normal workweek, written every half hour, to see where your hours go before you try to fit more in. Then hand off or drop the work that doesn’t serve your top priorities, and book calendar time for the work that does, while your week still has open hours.

When long days still leave you behind on your top priorities, staying later only adds hours of the same mix of work.

You win time back by dropping tasks, not by doing all of them faster, and memory is a poor guide to which tasks to drop. Looking back on a week, you’ll remember the long meetings and the big deadline, not the five-minute favors, status emails, and file searches that filled the time in between.

So log the week as it happens. Pick a week without a holiday or a deadline crunch, and every 30 minutes write down what you did in the half hour before, interruptions included. A notebook works, or use Clockify, a time tracker whose free plan times each task and breaks the week down in a report.

On Friday, sort every entry into three groups: work only you can do, work someone else could do, and work nobody needs. Hand the second group to a teammate or a direct report, or automate it with an email template or a scheduled report. Stop doing the third, after you check with your manager that no one relies on it.

Take a manager whose log shows nine hours a week in status meetings, including two hour-long weekly check-ins where people only share updates. She replaces those two hours of meetings with a shared written update that takes each person 15 minutes a week to post and read, so she and every attendee get an hour and 45 minutes back each week.

Before you leave on Friday, book time on next week’s calendar for your top two or three priorities, so new meetings get scheduled around those blocks.

Jason Blum and James Wan Discuss Why Horror Is Hollywood’s Hottest Genre and How ‘Obsession’ Hit Big at Blumhouse Atomic’s Business of Fear

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Blumhouse Atomic bosses Jason Blum and James Wan took a victory lap Wednesday at their Business of Fear panel, during which they discussed horror’s banner year at the box office and why “Obsession” became the breakout hit of the summer.

But before the horror talk began, they had to address the name change. Blum’s Blumhouse and Wan’s Atomic Monster merged in 2024, and the combined labels were therefore known as Blumhouse Atomic Monster. Recently, in early November, Blumhouse Atomic Monster rebranded to just Blumhouse Atomic. Blum explained they rebranded to give a more definitive identity to the overarching banner of the two companies, while also clearing the way for both distinct labels to take credit for their work. Related Stories Skydance Plans Launch of Bundled Paramount+, HBO Max in Early 2027 Cineplex Boards Sanfic Title ‘La Residencia’ From ‘Algo Azul' Director Mariel García Spooner

Wan added that Blumhouse Atomic Monster was also “a bit of a mouthful.”

“As Jason pointed out, it really still is Blumhouse movies and Atomic Monster movies,” Wan added. “We’re still making the movies as one company, but still under two labels. We put out a bunch of movies, and so we have to sort of divide it. Some movies play better at Atomic Monster, some movies play better at Blumhouse. At the start of movies, even though it’s going to say Blumhouse Atomic Presents, our logos will still stand individually.”

It was then on to horror’s stellar year. Moderator Ray Subers, head of film at NRG, presented Blum and Wan with a stat that theater attendance for horror films in 2026 went up 7% from pre-pandemic levels, while attendance for all other genre films (action, sci-fi, rom-coms) decreased. Blum theorized this was because watching horror on the big screen is “participatory,” and that audiences craved a “collective experience” after emerging from COVID lockdown.

“I think that after COVID, audiences ran back to sporting events and ran back to concerts, but they had been fed movies,” Blum said. “Kids had been fed movies so much at home that it took longer to discover and remember the movies [in the theater]. And I think horror specifically, if there’s any genre that’s close to a concert or a sporting event, I think horror is more than anything else because it’s so participatory. I think movies generally, but horror specifically, are kind of joining sports and concerts as a great way to get out of your house and have a collective, fun, exciting thing to do that doesn’t involve your phone.”

The conversation then shifted to “Obsession.” Blum joined as an executive producer shortly after Focus Features acquired the film, which, from a budget of less than $1 million, grossed over $500 million worldwide. Blum said he thinks “Obsession” took off for two reasons. The first is because young people can relate to the “universal feeling” of being “obsessed or having someone obsessed with them.” The second reason was that director Curry Barker sharpened his filmmaking skills on YouTube and social media before coming to Hollywood.

“I think that he has a dialogue with his audience, which he has honed over years and years and years of putting little pieces of content on YouTube and watching his audience react and adjusting to it,” Blum said. “The best way I can describe it is it almost feels like when you’re watching the movie, Curry is like whispering in your ear little funny things or like you’re talking to this invisible hand that’s directing the movie in a way that’s very unique.”

Wan added that the success of “Obsession” has inspired a whole new generation of filmmakers wanting to make scary movies, which reminds him of when he was trying to break into the industry with “Saw.”

“That stigma is gone. Horror has crossed over from the black sheep of the household, right?” Wan said. “I really do credit the younger generation with accepting the genre in a big way and not turning your nose down on it in a snooty way. When I was doing ‘Saw’ people looked down on it, right? And it was only a certain demographic that would come out and support the films that we made. But today, like today, it is cool and hip to be making horror films.”

NFL slams NBC's Mike Florio, Chris Simms for 'offensive' Mike Vrabel joke

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The NFL is not happy with one of its primary media partners.

During a recent episode of "Pro Football Talk Live" on NBC Sports, Mike Florio and Chris Simms made a joke at the expense of New England Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel that included a thinly veiled reference to Adolf Hitler, although neither mentioned his name.

"He got the German mustache to go along with that right now, too," Simms said during a Nov. 17 edition of the show while discussing the Patriots' 35-10 loss to the Detroit Lions on Sunday in Munich, Germany. "So he was hitting all themes there over in Germany."

"You gotta be careful trimming that thing," Florio said. "Yeah, if you're in Germany, you gotta be careful trimming the sides of that thing."

Between a lot of laughs, Simms and Florio kept the bit going for a few more seconds, although an off-air producer must have appeared to wish the duo didn't go down that road.

"It was eighty years ago," Florio said. "Let us have some fun, for crying out loud."

The exchange did not sit well within NFL headquarters.

"We found the remarks inappropriate and offensive," the NFL said in a statement to USA TODAY Sports from executive vice president of communications, policy and public affairs Jeff Miller

Both Florio and Simms contribute to NBC's elbow coverage of "Sunday Night Football." Simms had previously been part of "Football Night in America" but was removed from the show this offseason. The duo apologized Thursday.

"On yesterday's (Nov. 17) PFT Live show, we made comments that some found offensive and we apologize," Simms and Florio said in a joint statement via NBC Sports.

Why Peabody Energy Stock Slumped Today

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

An analyst made a deep cut to his price target on the coal company.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Peabody Energy ›

Investors struggled to find the energy to be positive about Peabody Energy (BTU -1.45%) on Wednesday. An analyst tracking the storied coal company made a significant cut to his price target, which dampened sentiment toward the stock. Its shares closed the day nearly 1.5% lower.

That move came before the market open and was made by Freedom Broker's Vitaly Kononov. He now believes Peabody stock is destined for $22 per share, down considerably from his former level of $30.

Despite the deep cut, Kononov remained bullish on the coal company's future, as he maintained his buy recommendation.

According to reports, the prognosticator's new take centered around Centurion, Peabody's flagship metallurgical coal mine in Australia. Kononov noted that the production outlook for the play has been cut three times so far this year. The analyst also considers coal prices to be vulnerable.

Centurion is rightfully considered to be Peabody's most important play, so how it develops will make a major difference to the company's results. What I'd be nervous about if I were a company shareholder is the future of the energy industry, an important client base for Peabody's thermal coal business, given Peabody's continued (and deep) involvement in the segment. For numerous reasons, the energy sector has become greener over the years, and I think that's a permanent shift.

I'm bearish on coal companies generally for this reason, and as such I'm bearish on Peabody.

Warren Buffett Expects Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA, BRKB) Stock to Drop About 50% Again Someday. Here's What He Says to Do Then.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Berkshire's stock has already been roughly halved (or more) three times. That should give investors confidence.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Berkshire Hathaway ›

When Warren Buffett talks, most savvy investors listen. Last year, in his now-annual Thanksgiving letter, he said:

Our stock price will move capriciously, occasionally falling 50% or so as has happened three times in 60 years under present management.

Buffett followed that sentence with this one:

Don't despair; America will come back and so will Berkshire shares.

Buffett was at the helm of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.71%) (BRKB -2.07%) for about 60 years, and grew the company by a remarkable annual average of nearly 20% over that period. Now 96 years old, he has since stepped away from that top job and is chairman emeritus.

Buffett actually detailed the three 50%-ish drops earlier, in his 2017 letter to shareholders, along with a 37% drop. I'm including that data in the table below. Note that it refers to the company's Class A shares, not the Class B shares that most shareholders likely own.

Both classes of stock generally move in tandem. As I write this, Class A shares are trading at around $878,500 apiece, and Class B shares near $587. Few of us could afford a share of Class A stock, which is part of the reason that Class B shares exist.

There are lessons to be drawn from that table that apply to much of our investing, not just to Berkshire Hathaway stock. For starters, note how the lengths of the declines vary -- from a few weeks to a year or two.

That's a great reminder that whenever the stock market corrects or crashes -- and the S&P 500 has actually been generally declining over the past month or so, down about 3% -- it may not stay down very long. (Berkshire Class A shares, meanwhile, are up about 17.5% over the past month!)

Note, too, that Berkshire shares and stocks, in general, tend to bounce back quite well, either quickly or eventually. Look at that 50.7% drop nearly 20 years ago, from $147,000 per share to $72,400. And look where the shares are now -- $878,500.

The folks at The Hartford Funds have offered some handy insights about market downturns, noting that:

So take Buffett's advice to heart. Expect occasional downturns and plan to ride them out while seeking bargains. Only keep money in stocks that you won't need for five, if not more, years, so that you can ride out a downturn.

Why The World's Debut Boeing 747 Was Built In Half The Time Of Other Aircraft Of Its Size In 1968

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Boeing 747 has earned its title as one of the most iconic airliners ever built for several reasons. It was the first widebody aircraft, easily distinguishable by a humped second deck, a key enabler in opening up aviation to the masses, and it enjoyed roughly fifty years of production. But equally as impressive as the Queen of the Skies itself is how it came about.

Boeing took the 747 from the drawing board to rollout remarkably quickly. That is, notably faster than the Airbus A380 program took to churn out its first model after officially launching, and quicker than both Boeing and Airbus took with their latest widebodies. What is more, Boeing pulled it off decades ago in the late 1960s.

Just 29 months separated the 747 project’s official launch and the rollout of the first model. Boeing announced on April 13, 1966, that it would build the 490-seat passenger transport that would come to be known as the 747. Exactly two years, five months, and 17 days later, on September 30, 1968, the first 747-100 ceremoniously rolled off the production line at a new purpose-built assembly plant in Everett, Washington.

Less than five months later, on February 9, 1969, the aircraft took off for its maiden flight. Test pilots Jack Waddell and Brien Wygle, accompanied by flight engineer Jess Wallick, took the controls, and the jumbo jet passed the major test with flying colors, paving the way for its introduction into service just under a year later in January 1970.

All told, that saw the Queen of the Skies go from a mere concept to carrying passengers in under four years. While that is no doubt a rapid turnaround by today’s standards, the reasons behind the pace are plentiful and clear-cut.

Behind it all was keen interest from Pan American World Airways, which had set out to source a jet two and a half times larger than the existing Boeing 707 against the backdrop of an increasingly busy market. Though the industry was mulling whether supersonic flight would come to dominate in the years ahead, there remained appeal for a huge, versatile airliner that could handle cargo operations rather than passenger services in the future, if needed.

As it turned out, Boeing had already looked into something similar, having lost out in a 1965 military transport design competition to the Lockheed C-5 Galaxy. Although the 747 was ultimately largely built from scratch, research into high-bypass engine technology and the thinking behind the 747’s distinctive hump can be traced back to the military project bid. As such, when Pan Am head Juan Trippe and then-Boeing president William Allen met that same year for initial discussions about the 747, something of a basis was already there.

It was Trippe himself who is said to have pushed so heavily for the 747. He had spent years beforehand attempting to open up aviation to the masses, overseeing the introduction of the world’s first economy fare in 1958, for instance. Given that an ambitious project like the 747 would only aid in reaching that goal, Trippe reportedly told Allen in 1965: “If you’ll build it, I’ll buy it.” By April 1966, the commitment was solidified through a $525 million deal — worth almost $5.5 billion today — for 25 of the new jumbo jets. This set an ambitious deadline of 1969 for the first delivery, though.

The 747 transcended aviation and became an iconic silhouette.

Nonetheless, Boeing did not waste any time in dwelling on the task at hand. After signing the contract with Pan Am, the design team of just a few hundred workers conceptualizing and writing up requirements for the 747 ballooned to 4,500 almost instantaneously. These workers, who would later become part of a group known as The Incredibles, were led by Joe Sutter, who would similarly earn the title of the Father of the 747 over time.

Sutter himself enjoyed a 40-year-long career at Boeing, although he recalled in a 2013 interview fearing for his job as he and his team rushed to get the 747 ready. Without public backing, Boeing found itself racking up debt on the back of the project, leaving Sutter under pressure to cut jobs. Instead, he requested hundreds more engineers. “I figured that was the day I lost my job at Boeing,” he said, “but I went to work the next day, and nobody said anything, so I kept working."

According to Boeing, some 50,000 employees ultimately ended up working on the jumbo jet over the course of its development cycle. Armed with an eye-watering 75,000 drawings, physical assembly of the first model got underway in September 1967, a year prior to its initial presentation to the world and only 16 months before the first flight. All of this, of course, required a specialized facility for production to actually be carried out.

Such a factory did not exist when Boeing first unveiled plans for the 747, so it had to be built almost in conjunction with the first model. Among the battles Sutter fought in his bid to steer the project to a timely conclusion was one over where his team would be based. Faced with the proposal of being sent to California, Sutter argued against it and insisted his engineers stay close to where those who would eventually build the aircraft would work.

This turned out to be Everett, which was far from Boeing’s first choice to house the 747 program initially, but prevailed on similar fears that key personnel would not relocate in great numbers to a new facility elsewhere. Regardless of where the 747 would be built, a new building would have to be constructed to accommodate such a vast aircraft and all the equipment needed to build it.

Under the tight deadline set by the deal with Pan Am, Boeing set about clearing forest and then building the facility in 1967. Work began just before assembly of the first 747 started, but the two efforts overlapped, meaning the prototype was under construction in an incomplete factory for a period. When the former was eventually completed, it took the crown as the largest building in the world by volume — a title it retains to this day and a reflection of the mammoth undertaking by Boeing to get the project off the ground.

The original designs for the Boeing 747 were made with a different purpose in mind. Find out how the 747 came to be as we know it today.

Naturally, such an effort required vast financial backing, and it took a monumental amount of risk on Boeing’s part to get the first 747 successfully turned around at such a pace. Boeing had secured $525 million — or $21 million per aircraft — under its deal with Pan Am. This covered barely half of the program's total $1 billion costas it transpired; however, adjusted for inflation, it would exceed $10 billion today.

As a result, Boeing faced financial pressure and was forced to turn to the banks, which explains Sutter being asked to cut engineers midway through the program. He said in 2013 that “it was a big gamble,” adding, “I think the only reason that it happened is that Bill Allen was a forward-seeking guy, and Juan Trippe from Pan Am also had that characteristic.”

Despite racking up debt, the gamble paid off, and the 747 was a resounding success. Not only did the first model get built and delivered on time, but the factory Boeing built for the jumbo jet has enjoyed decades of service since. Some 1,574 747s rolled out of the site over the life of the program, culminating in the delivery of a final 747-8 Freighter variant to Atlas Air in 2023. Notably, this saw production of the 747 end just a year before that of its primary challenger, the A380, despite its initial introduction decades earlier.

The A380 is an impressive feat of engineering in its own right. The industry moved in a different direction, and while Boeing hit a sweet spot with the 747, the appetite for huge quad jets was by no means as strong as it was at the A380’s commercial entry in 2007. All told, this saw the 254th and final A380 completed in 2021.

Airbus grappled with a string of delays between officially launching the A380 in December 2000, unveiling a prototype in January 2005, and then making the initial delivery to Singapore Airlines in 2007. How severely these impacted the A380’s long-term success is up for debate. Either way, the A380’s stretched timeline offers some context for how impressive Boeing’s effort was in getting the 747 up and running so quickly.

And it is not just the A380. Boeing itself took three years and six months to show off its first 777 after launching the program in October 1990. Its 787, despite going from concept to prototype in three years and three months, then took an additional two years and five months to first take off in December 2009. As for Airbus, the A350 and its answer to the clean-sheet 787 went from launch to initial rollout in six years and five months. Similarly, the 777X project took more than five years after launching in 2013 to produce a prototype, with certification still not secured today.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Dividend Stocks for 2027: Kimberly-Clark vs. McDonald's

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Kimberly-Clark trades at a noticeably lower multiple than McDonald's, but the franchise giant's richer price tag raises a question about which premium is justified.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 8:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kimberly-Clark ›

Investors often seek stability in market giants that dominate daily life. Deciding between a consumer essentials powerhouse like Kimberly-Clark (KMB -0.84%) and a global restaurant titan like McDonald's (MCD -0.65%) requires weighing income against growth.

Kimberly-Clark provides essential hygiene products that consumers buy regardless of the economic climate. McDonald's leverages a massive franchise model to generate consistent cash flow through the fast-food market. Comparing these two involves looking at how defensive positioning in household goods stacks up against the high-margin, scalable nature of the global restaurant business.

Kimberly-Clark sells essential hygiene and health products to households and professionals among consumer staples stocks worldwide. Its brands, including Huggies and Kleenex, reach customers in more than 175 countries through supermarkets and e-commerce platforms. Walmart (WMT -0.13%) is its largest customer, accounting for nearly 16% of net sales, and customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $16.6 billion. The company reported net income of close to $3.3 billion during this same timeframe. Kimberly-Clark has total cash of $1.8 billion, with total debt of $6.5 billion. Over a 12-month trailing period, it has operating cash flow of $3.3 billion.

McDonald's operates a global fast-food empire by primarily franchising its locations to independent owners rather than running them directly. This model allows for high net margins compared to many traditional retail-focused businesses. It also maintains a delivery partnership with Uber Technologies (UBER -0.07%) to help boost volume and customer loyalty across more than a dozen global markets.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $27.8 billion, which shows revenue growth of nearly 5.8%. Net income for this period was roughly $9 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to turn sales into significant bottom-line profit. The steady expansion of the franchise system and delivery infrastructure supports these consistent financial results.

Kimberly-Clark faces intense competition from other branded household products and lower-priced private label manufacturers. It is sensitive to price changes in volatile commodities like cellulose fiber and petroleum-based materials used in manufacturing. Additionally, the company must manage the integration of its acquisition of Kenvue while navigating global regulatory and sustainability reporting requirements.

McDonald's deals with fierce competition and depends heavily on the financial health of its individual franchisees. Labor shortages and rising wage costs can pressure the system, while food safety concerns or public perception issues can damage the global brand reputation.

McDonald's carries a significantly higher premium than Kimberly-Clark based on both its forward price-to-earnings (P/E) and its price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, which measures market cap relative to sales over the past twelve months.

Both Kimberly-Clark and McDonald's are well-run companies, and while McDonald's is known for its singular brand, Kimberly-Clark is known for multiple brands, ranging from Kleenex to Band-Aid. Both are also dividend stocks, helping investors generate more income. Kimberly-Clark's dividend offers a higher yield of 4.7%, while McDonald's is 2.8%.

That said, between the two, I'd rather own Kimberly-Clark. That's because in tight economic times, Kimberly-Clark offers items people will keep buying, whereas people may limit how often they go out to eat. We are seeing a bit of that play out as McDonald's figures out how to keep prices lower amid rising costs. For its 2026 third-quarter results, restaurant sales in the United States decreased by 0.2%.

In comparison, Kimberly-Clark has more predictable demand, which is nice to have with all the uncertainty swirling around the markets. In the first nine months of 2026, Kimberly-Clark reported a 1% increase in net sales to $12.4 billion. That 1% increase may not sound exciting, but again, it's about consistency. Also, the acquisition of the consumer healthcare company Kenvue gives Kimberly-Clark even more essential products to sell, adding brands like Tylenol and Listerine to its portfolio. While consumers may cut back on going out to eat and ordering burgers and fries, they won't hesitate to buy Huggies or Tylenol if they need them.

Convert to a Roth IRA After a Market Drop – Same Shares, Smaller Tax Bill

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

If you expect the same or a higher tax rate in retirement, convert traditional IRA or 401(k) money to a Roth after a market drop. You owe income tax on what the shares are worth the day you convert, so the lower price shrinks the bill, and any rebound grows tax-free inside the Roth.

Say every dollar you convert is taxed at your 22% federal rate, and shares in your traditional IRA worth $50,000 in January fall to $40,000 in a downturn. Converting them after the drop, rather than in January, adds $40,000 to your taxable income instead of $50,000, so the federal tax comes to $8,800 instead of $11,000.

If the shares climb back to $50,000, your Roth holds the same $50,000 a January conversion would have left you, for $2,200 less tax. That $10,000 rebound comes out tax-free after 59½, once five tax years have passed since you first put money in a Roth.

Whether converting pays at all depends on your expected tax rate in retirement, which also decides between a Roth and a traditional IRA. Conversions have no income limit, which is what lets high earners use a backdoor Roth.

With a 401(k), convert inside the plan if it offers that option, or roll the money into a Roth IRA once the plan lets you take it out, such as after you leave the job or reach 59½.

Pay the tax from savings outside the IRA or 401(k), with an estimated tax payment or extra withholding from your paycheck. Tax withheld from the conversion itself never reaches the Roth, and if you’re under 59½, it counts as an early withdrawal, which can cost a 10% penalty.

Convert in pieces, too. A conversion made since 2018 can’t be undone, so if the shares keep falling after you convert, you still owe tax on what they were worth that day. Spread the conversion across a few dates, and stop each year’s total at the top of your current tax bracket, since every dollar past it is taxed at the next rate.

Each conversion also starts its own five-year clock, counted from January 1 of the year you convert, and taking converted money out before it runs out can cost anyone under 59½ a 10% penalty. So convert only money you can leave alone for five years.

For the Roth IRA that receives the converted money, compare the best places to open an IRA.

Procrastination Isn’t Laziness – Shrink the First Step to Five Minutes

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

You put off tasks that feel too big, too vague, or likely to go badly, and waiting to feel motivated won’t change that. Beat procrastination by making the start easy. Pick a first step you can finish in five minutes, tell someone your deadline, and aim for a rough version, not a perfect one.

Putting those tasks off isn’t laziness, but it still costs you. A bill paid late can bring a late fee, and work handed in late can cost you your boss’s trust.

You might clean the whole kitchen to avoid writing one report. That’s real effort, spent on anything except the task that makes you uneasy.

The fix is to make starting the task easier than avoiding it.

Take a freelancer who is weeks late on her quarterly estimated tax payment, the tax she pays the IRS four times a year because no employer withholds it. The IRS figures its underpayment penalty on how much is unpaid and for how long, so every week she waits adds to it.

She commits to five minutes of gathering last quarter’s invoices. With them in one pile, the rest goes quickly, and she pays that afternoon.

Choose the task you’ve put off longest, write down its five-minute first step, and start a five-minute timer.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Trading Treasuries (nominal and TIPS)

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

First thing I try is to refresh the quotes screen; sometimes the quote is stale by the time you click Preview. If you do this on the order entry (Trade) screen (not the full DOB screen), you need to re-enter the quantity to get the correct price.
I suspect a stale quote is a problem with any system that shows you a market quote. If you wait too long to execute the trade, the market can move against you, possibly by only a tiny amount, and then the order fails. One solution would be for the system to let you buy at the market at the time of execution, although you'd risk a big market move in a short amount of time (a low risk IMO).
This happened to me again today at Fidelity in buying qty 1 of the Jul 2036 TIPS'; refreshing the quotes then reentering the qty enabled me to place the trade, which was executed immediately.

This almost never happens to me at Schwab--can't remember that last time it did. I think this is because Schwab provides the min qty 1 quotes from its own inventory, and prices are relatively stable for those.
